199. GEORGE DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of November, 1 handkerchief, value 6d., the goods of John Waugh, from his person.
JOHN WAUGH. I am an agent. On the 20th of November, between nine and ten o'clock in the evening, I was in Farringdon-street - I felt my handkerchief going, turned, and seized the prisoner, who was about two feet from me, in company with another person who has escaped - I did not see my handkerchief in the prisoner's possession, but it was brought to me by another person.
PETER JACKSON. On the 20th of November I saw the prisoner following the prosecutor; I saw him put his hand into the prosecutor's pocket, and take his handkerchief out; the prosecutor seized him; I ran up, and the prosecutor said, "He has taken my handkerchief" - I said,"Sir, he has," and I assisted in taking him to the watch-house; we were surrounded by a gang, who tried to get him away, and the prisoner kicked me about the feet and legs - I carried the marks for some time.
JOSEPH COOPER. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and received the handkerchief from the prosecutor.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-200]
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
200. JOHN WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of November, 1 watch, value 16l.; 1 watch-chain, value 3l.; 1 seal, value 20s., and 1 watch-key, value 1d., the goods of David Jones, from his person.
DAVID JONES. I reside at Peckham, but when in London, at No. 15, Size-lane. On the afternoon of the 26th of November, I was going through a little passage leading from St. Michael's-alley into Bell-yard; I had a coach cloak on my arm, and an umbrella in my right hand - the cloak hung down to my feet, and the moment I turned from St. Michael's church-yard into the little passage, I was surrounded by six or seven men, some before, and some behind me, pushing me; I turned to the prisoner, who was before me, and said, "Why can't you go on quietly?" the prisoner then came in front of me, and put his left arm across my body - I then had some suspicion, and I made a little room for myself; I put my hand down, and my watch was gone - they then separated, and the prisoner went back into St. Michael's-alley; I called Stop thief! and he was stopped - I got hold of him by the Jamaica coffee-house, and seized him by the collar - the rest of the party came up - they endeavoured to get him away, and some of them gave me a blow on the head; I saw the prisoner in communication with another of the party, but having no hand disengaged at the moment, I could not take hold of his other hand - they gave another rush he broke from me, and turned into Bell-yard; I immediately went after him and attempted to seize him, but he got away, and dropped his hat - I still followed, and he was taken in Finch-lane.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You saw the prisoner in the watch-house afterwards? A. Yes; I did not then say that I knew him by his swivel eye; I said by the cast in his eye - there was a great rush of people about me.
COURT. Q.How long had you had him in hold? A. I suppose a minute; I had a full opportunity of viewing his face; there was a gas-lamp at the end of the passage, and a hair-dresser's-shop at the corner; I have no doubt of his person.
PETER HALE. I was in the George and Vulture tavern, in St. Michael's-alley, on the 26th of November, a little after five o'clock; I heard an alarm, and saw the prisoner running - I caught hold of him, and delivered him to Mr. Jones; the prisoner's companions came round me, and gave me a blow on the back of my head - I speedily recovered, and saw them struggling at the corner of the alley; the prisoner dropped his hat, and ran - his companions got between Mr. Jones and him, and they got away; I had such a view of him as to be able to speak to him positively - I should think he was taken within three or four minutes; I had a good observation of him while his hat was off.
JURY. Q.Were there many persons passing? A. There were men and boys passing.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q.How many persons were in the street altogether? A. I cannot say, there might be ten or a dozen; it was darkish, but the lamps were lighted - the place was light enough for a person to see; I took hold of the prisoner, and no one else -I did not observe any thing of a cast in his eye; I heard Mr. Jones say something about his eye here, but I never heard him say so before; there were from twelve to twenty persons in the watch-house.
COURT. Q.How long afterwards did you see the prisoner in the watch-house? A.Immediately as he was brought in.
MR. JONES. It was a gold watch; a gold seal and chain - I should think they were worth 20l.
JOHN PARKER. I heard the cry of Stop thief! as I was entering St. Michael's-alley; a person in the crowd said,"That is him without a hat;" I followed the prisoner, overtook him at the end of Finch-lane, and secured him -I took him to the watch-house; his hat was brought in, and he said, "That is my hat;" there were several persons there - he was running from Cornhill towards Threadneedle-street.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. He was taken to the watch-house? A. Yes - his hat was brought in, and he owned it; his observation to me was, "I am not the thief;" Mr. Jones was sitting in a chair in the watch-house - the prisoner said, "I am not the man," and Mr. Jones said, "I know you by your swivel eye," or some such observation - the prisoner said he had no cast in his eye, and he appealed to me; I said I had known him for some time, and there was a peculiarity in his eye-sight - there is a fulness about his eyes; they stand rather out - if you have ever seen a person of his description, I should think you would know him again; he was running fast - I had known him for some time.
Witnesses for the Defence.
CHARLES HARRIS. I am an agent for Scotch ale - I live in Cornwall-road, Stamford-street. I have known the prisoner five years; he owed me a little money. On Saturday week last, I met him in Gracechurch-street; I went across the road, and told him I thought he behaved in a very ungrateful manner - we had some angry words, and I told him if he did not pay me the money, or some part of it, I would give him in charge of an officer; I laid hold of him by his breast coat, and he walked with me as far as Bell-yard - he then put up his hand, knocked my hand off, and ran up Bell-yard; I followed him till he came to St. Michael's-alley - there was a crowd, who were hallooing Stop thief! or something of that kind; I saw the prisoner's hat fall from his head as he turned the corner.
COURT. Q. Did you see two persons with their hats off? A. No, only him.
THOMAS ANDERSON. I live at No. 29, Laurel-street, Chelsea; I deal in building materials - I do not know the prisoner. On Saturday evening, the 26th of November, I was with Mr. Hill, a solicitor; we went through Bell-yard, into St. Michael's-alley - there are a great many little alleys and turnings there, and there were a great many people - it was quite light; as I went into St. Michael's-alley there was a crush of people, and I was pushed a little way forward towards the wide part - I heard a gentleman say, "I have lost my watch, Stop thief!" several persons at that time passed me - I seized one, who appeared to be the suspected party - I exclaimed, "Here he is! I have got him!" I was then struck several times - I was struck over the nose, and the man I had hold of got away; he had himself struck me, but I do not know whether he struck me on the nose - he got away from me, and I followed him as well as I could; I dare say a dozen persons at least followed with me - I had an opportunity by the light of the court of observing the man I caught hold of, and I noticed that he had a cross-eye; he looked with one eye towards his nose - his right eye was fixed towards his nose, it was a squint; the prisoner certainly is not the man I took hold of; when that man got from me he went the way I had come, through the narrow part of St. Michael's-alley, through Bell-yard, and into Gracechurch-street; I followed him to the corner, and there lost sight of him.
COURT. Q. Did you see two men with their hats off? A. No, only one.
FRANCIS HILL. I am a solicitor, and live at No. 119, Fore-street, Cripplegate. I was in company with Mr. Anderson; just as we were coming from the narrow part into the wide part of St. Michael's-alley, Mr. Jones, the prosecutor, called out that he had been robbed of his watch - several people passed at full speed; Mr. Jones followed a person who was running, and a porter in a white apron stopped him, opposite the George and Vulture door - a violent scuffle ensued, and the porter was knocked down; the man he had hold of broke loose, and Mr. Anderson caught at him with one hand - he broke from his hold; he caught him again, and held him nearly half a minute - a scuffle ensued, and several persons struck Mr. Anderson; among the rest the man he had hold of struck him, broke from him, and ran down Bellyard; he then eluded our pursuit round some coaches - we returned back the same way, and heard that a man had been taken in Cornhill, which was the contrary way to that which the man we saw had run - I had observed that that man squinted; he had a strong cast in his eye; there was a gas-lamp over his head, and he turned round under the gas-lamp - I had a full view of his face; the prisoner certainly is not the person.
COURT. Q. Did you go to the watch-house? A. No; I did not see Charles Harris - I was subpoenaed to come here.
COURT to THOMAS ANDERSON. Q. When were you first applied to to give evidence? A. On Monday, I had occasion to come to town, and passing the Mansion-house I went in to know how the young man had gone on about the watch: as I had been present when he was taken, I made the observation, "This is not the man," I saw his mother and sister, and gave them my address - I said to Mr. Hill "I might have been taken in the same way."
JOHN GREY BROOMHILL. I am beadle of Whitechapel. I was in the watch-house, and saw Peter Hall there, and Mr. Jones, the prosecutor - I remember Mr. Jones told the prisoner he knew him by his swivel eye - my attention was called to him from that circumstance; I looked at the prisoner's eyes, but I could not perceive it - the prisoner denied any knowledge of Mr. Jones' robbery.
COURT. Q.Had you any thing to do with the apprehension of the prisoner? A. Yes; I took him in Finch-lane, about half-past five o'clock, on Saturday week; a great many persons were following him - I had no knowledge of him before; he had no hat on at the time - I do not recollect seeing any thing of Mr. Harris, Mr. Anderson, or Mr. Hill - I can only recognize Mr. Jones, the ticket-porter, and the street-keeper; the other persons might have been there, and I not see them - there was a rush of people; I saw the prisoner running; I crossed, and took him - he said, "What do you stop me for? I have lost my hat in pursuit of the person;" I did not see him go near any coaches; I do not know who brought the hat in.
JURY to MR. JONES. Q. Did you lose sight of the man after losing your watch? A. I never lost sight of him from the time he took the watch out of my pocket till I was pushed down, after the rescue, and after I had followed him the second time; he lost his hat between the time of the robbery and my losing sight of him in Bellyard - I only lost sight of him, because I could not runso fast; I was a little agitated, but I should think not more than four or five minutes elapsed from the time I lost my watch till the prisoner was taken - I left my residence at five o'clock, the whole of it was over, and I went home by the six o'clock coach.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18311201-201]
201. WILLIAM MURRAY was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of October, 1 watch, value 3l.; 1 key, value 6d., and 1 watch-ribbon, value 1d., the goods of Joseph Nicholas, from his person.
THOMAS TOOLE. I live at No. 20, Hatfield-street, St. Luke's, and am a shoemaker. On Monday afternoon, the 3rd of October, I was at the corner of Gracechurch-street - there was a placard there about the Lord Mayor's election; there was a crowd there, and several persons surrounded the prosecutor and rifled him about - one of them, who I think was the prisoner, took his watch - he was dressed exactly like the prisoner, and he was among those who were hustling the prosecutor; I saw the watch taken from the prosecutor by a person, who I believe to be the prisoner - he put it into his frock coat pocket, behind the party, and then separated directly; some went on one side, and some on the other - I stopped for a moment - the prosecutor missed his watch, and the beadle (Sheppard) crossed over to know what was the matter; I called him; and said, "Mr. Sheppard, there is a person gone along with a frock coat on, who has got the watch;" I followed, went up to the prisoner, and took the watch from his coat pocket behind - Mr. Sheppard collared him, several persons came up, and said, "That is not the person - let him go, and see for the person who has got the watch;" I said, "I have got the watch" - Sheppard let him go, and laid hold of me; the prisoner then got away - I gave the watch to the street-keeper; I afterwards met the prisoner in Lincoln's Inn-fields - there was a crowd, and I saw him go round it several times - he then walked in the direction in which I did; I met a Policeman, and he was taken - I had not an opportunity of seeing the prisoner's face at the moment the street-keeper took hold of him; and I took the watch - I saw his side face.
COURT. Q.You said you came in front of him? A. No, my Lord, I did not.
Q. Is not this your signature? A. Yes; (reads) -"I went in front of him, and looked him full in the face"- I might have said so, but I saw his side face, and I thought it was the same thing - I am not sure the prisoner is the person who took the watch; there were one or two persons dressed like him.
SAMUEL SHEPPARD. I am a ward-officer of Bishopsgate. On the 3rd of October I was in Bishopsgate-street, near the Old London tavern; I saw Toole there - he pointed out a man to me; I have no doubt the prisoner is the man be pointed out - I took him by the collar - Toole was then behind me - he held up the watch, and said, "I have got the watch," and kept drawing back - a mob broke on my arm; I made a dart, caught hold of Toole, and by the assistance of another man I got him into a passage of a house, and here he gave up the watch - this is it; I am certain the prisoner is the man - I saw him in custody in about ten days afterwards.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q.This occurred on the 3rd? A. Yes, and I saw him in custody in about ten days afterwards; I cannot swear it was not a fortnight afterwards - I cannot swear that it was not on the 29th or 30th that I saw him in custody; I have not the summons with me - it was all in the same month; my memory is rather doubtful in that respect; I was rather anxious to take hold of Toole - I suspected him at the time he drew back with the watch - it appeared the more anxious I was to take him the more he drew back; I never saw the prisoner with the watch - I do not know whether Toole looked into the prisoner's face; there was an outcry raised about the watch at the time Toole showed it, and he appeared to be trying to get from me.
WILLIAM BONSER. On the afternoon of the 3rd of October I was in Gracechurch-street, and was surrounded by a number of persons, but I cannot swear to the prisoner at all; I had my watch attempted to be taken, and then I heard Mr. Nicholas say he had lost his watch; I was standing by Sheppard, and heard Toole say, "I know the man who has got the watch;" the officer then went into Bishopsgate-street, and about five yards beyond the London tavern there is a gateway; I there saw the officer take a person by the collar - I do not know who the person was, but I saw Toole put his hand into his pocket and take out a watch; there was then a cry on', and I heard some person say, "He is one of them;" Sheppard let the person go, and he walked off very deliberately - I was surprised that he should take hold of a person walking as he did.
Cross-examined. Q. You were a little shy of them? A. Yes; I had not known Toole before - it appeared that he was edging off with the watch.
JOSEPH NICHOLAS. I am a merchant, and live in Church-court, Clement's-lane, Lombard-street. On the afternoon of the 3rd of October I was at the corner of Gracechurch-street, and was surrounded by a number of persons - I lost my watch, and it was returned to me, by order of the Lord Mayor, by the street-keeper.
Cross-examined. Q. Did any thing remarkable occur before you missed your watch? A. No; there was a placard, and my watch was whipt away in a moment - there was nothing touching my hand or arms; no one held my arms.
COURT. Q. What happened to you when the gang was about you? A Nothing, but when I got out of the crowd I missed my watch; I was hustled.
SAMUEL SHEPPARD re-examined. I delivered up the watch - I said the prisoner was the man I had hold of, but I never saw the watch in his possession.
MR. PHILLIPS to THOMAS TOOLE. Q.Have you sworn to day what you swore before the Magistrate? A. Yes, as near as I know - I there stated that the prisoner was the man; I was not asked to swear positively - I was asked if he was the person, and I said Yes; I was not asked if I looked into his face - I did not say I looked full in his face; I am not an officer - when Sheppard went down to the office the officers told him who I was, which removed his doubts about me, and he said, "Why did you not tell me that?" I have been several times examined in a Court of Justice - I was examined this morning; I was examined once last Session - I will not swear that I was nothere the Session before that; I do not think I have been here twenty-five times - I think I first began to be a witness about five years ago, when my father and I had both been robbed; I have got about 2l., perhaps, within this last twelve months for my expences, about 6s. a Session; I never said to any one that I knew the whole set of them, and could pull them up.
Q. Did you ever say so to this gentleman? A. I said I knew a good many of the parties; I did not say I knew the whole of them - I said I had seen a great many of them in the City several times; I might have said that I knew the whole of them, and could bring them up - my reason for going back with the watch was, I was afraid of having a knock down, and the people said, "That is not the man, look after the man that has got the watch," and I saw several of the gang coming towards me.
MR. BONSER. I saw Toole in the watch-house; he told me he knew the whole of them, and could bring them all up.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18311201-202]
202. JAMES REEVES was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of November, 20 lbs. weight of seed, value 20s., and 1 wooden box, value 6d., the goods of Thomas William Blackerville.
SECOND COUNT, stating it to be the property of Benjamin Worthy Horne and others.
THOMAS WILLIAM BLACKERVILLE. I am a porter at the Golden Cross, Charing-cross. On the 11th of November I was with my employers' cart, in St. Paul's church-yard - they are Benjamin Worthy Horne and several partners - my cart was standing by Doctors'-commons; I sent my boy down with a parcel to Doctors'-commons, and was standing about five yards from it - I did not see the prisoner get into the cart, but saw him coming out of it with the box on his shoulder, it contained seeds which came from France; I had to deliver it- the seeds have been given up to the owner; we have only a sample of them here.
JAMES WILMOT. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and have the box.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-203]
203. JACOB SIMMONDS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of November, 1 watch, value 20s.; 1 seal, value 6s.; 1 watch-key, value 2s., and 1 watch-chain, value 2s., the goods of James Saunders.
ELIZABETH CHARLOTTE SAUNDERS. I am the wife of James Saunders. On the morning of Saturday, the 12th of November, I observed the prisoner walking by the door several times, and while I was measuring some coals I saw him come out of a little room at the back of the shop; he went up to the counter, and asked for 1d. worth of apples - I went, and asked what he did in my room; he strongly denied it, and said he came in for 1d. worth of apples - I closed the door, and took hold of him - he gave me a blow on the face, got from me, and tried to get over the railings of the cellar; I seized him again - I sent one of my children for an officer; the watch had been under a glass in the room - I found it in the cellar, and I suppose while he was over the cellar he dropped it; this is it.
Prisoner. Q.Did not the person you served the coals to jump over the rails? A. I did not see that - my attention was taken up with you; I suspected you might be acquainted - I thought he pushed by me to lay his cloth down, that I might not observe the prisoner - the prisoner was never out of my sight, nor a yard from me; his hand was cut with the glass of the watch.
Prisoner's Defence. I went into the shop for 1d. worth of apples - I know nothing of the watch.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-204]

GEORGE SAVELL. I live in Moorfields. HENRY CORSE. I am the officer. This is the cloak.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Confined Three Months.

JOSEPH HAWKRIGG. I am an officer. I took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)

207. JOHN DONOVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of October, 4 1/2 lbs. of bacon, value 3s., the goods of George Foulkes; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE FOULKES. I lost a piece of bacon on Wednesday, the 26th of October, from my shop, in the Minories.
WILLIAM GODSELL. I live in Queen-street, Towerhill. I saw the prisoner take this piece of bacon from the prosecutor's window, on the 26th of October.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded poverty.

208. JAMES ROSE and JOHN MOLLOY were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of November, 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d., the goods of George Weblin, from his person.
GEORGE WEBLIN. I live in St. Mary-axe, and am a hair-dresser. On Sunday evening I was in Leadenhall-street with a female; she stopped, and I turned round and saw one of the prisoners drop my handkerchief - they were together with a third person, they were two or three yards from me when the handkerchief was dropped.
WILLIAM ARTHUR. On the 27th of November I was on duty in Leadenhall-street; I saw the prisoners and another person with them - I found this handkerchief under my feet, and saw Rose drop it; I seized these two, and the other person got away - they were walking close behind the prosecutor; Molloy attempted to get away - I called Stop thief! the prosecutor took him.
Molloy. Q.Where did you see me in company with Rose? A.Near the watch-house door; you and Rose were close together, arm-in-arm, and hand-in-hand, I may say; they were all three behind the prosecutor, busy in taking the handkerchief,
Rose's Defence. The handkerchief was hove at my feet.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Molloy's Defence. I was going to get a drink of water, and the officer said I was going to pick the gentleman's pocket - I was as good as six or seven yards before the gentleman.
ROSE - GUILTY. Aged 14.
MOLLOY - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Judgment Respited .

OLD COURT. WEDNESDAY, DECEMBER 7.
Third Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
209. JAMES GIBSON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of November, 1 waistcoat, value 7s., the goods of Peter Pige.
THOMAS STERN. I am servant to Peter Pige, pawnbroker, Church-street, Bethnal-green. On the 24th of November I received information that this waistcoat was taken from the iron bar before the window - I went out and seized the prisoner with it in Brick-lane; he was running.
ELIZA WEBB. I saw the prisoner snatch the waistcoat from the window, and gave information.
Prisoner's Defence. I was walking by with several people and the waistcoat came on my shoulder, whether it was put on or fell I do not know.
GUILTY. Aged 43. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-210]
210. LYDIA GOODWIN was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of October, 1 key, value 2d.; 2 half-crowns, 6 shillings, and sixpence, the property of James Speller, from his person.
JAMES SPELLER. I am a wire-worker. On the 29th of October, about seven o'clock, I received two half-crowns and fifteen shillings, for my wages - I met with a fellow-workman, and saw the prisoner and another girl in Whitechapel-road; I was quite sober - they accosted us, and we treated them with rum at a public-house, and when we got into the air, I became stupified and drunk - I am sure I had 12s. or 16s. when in the public-house in my left-hand pocket, and the two half-crowns were among them; I went no where with her, nor did I intend - when I came to myself, I found I was in the station-house; my money and a key were gone from the same pocket - the officer produced two half-crowns, 6s. 6d 1/2., and a key; I had some copper.
BENJAMIN HARRIS. I am an officer. I saw the prisoner and prosecutor together about one o'clock in the morning, walking towards Whitechapel; the man was very drunk, and fell on the crossing - I helped the prisoner to pick him up, and asked where he lived; he could not answer - the prisoner said he was her husband, and they only lived a few doors up the street; she had a small black bag in her hand, which she asked me to hold, as her shawl was apparently falling off - I thought it curious she should ask me to hold the bag, and asked what it contained; she said nothing but her handkerchief and gloves - I put my hand in and felt the handkerchief and gloves, but I am sure there was no money in it; the prosecutor was standing up - she then said she would manage him, and take him home; I left her, and presently heard money fall on the pavement - I met another officer, who went up with me, and found a halfpenny on the pavement; the prisoner said it was hers, and he gave it her - she came towards the top of the street, leaving the prosecutor laying on the pavement; I then secured her - the prosecutor was so tipsy he could say nothing, and I took them both to the station; I looked into her bag, and found two half-crowns, 6s. 6d., 2 1/2d., a farthing, and a key - I asked how the money came in her bag so quick; she said she had two half-crowns in a pocket in her stays, but the rest she could not account for - the prosecutor claimed the key; neither that nor the money were there in the first instance.
JAMES SPELLER. This is my key.
The prisoner delivered in a written Defence, stating that the Policeman had been drinking with her, and was quite intoxicated - that the prosecutor had stated he was so drunk that he did not know whether he had lost any money or not.
JAMES SPELLER. When I came to myself, I was asked if I had lost my money; I put my hand into my pocket and said I had - there is not a word of truth in her defence.
GUILTY. Aged 25. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18311201-211]
211. JAMES CLARK was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of November, 2 candlesticks, value 2s., the goods of Samuel Barber.
ELIZA BARBER. I am the wife of Samuel Barber, and keep a public-house in John-street, Golden-square. On the 26th of November the prisoner had a glass of beer and the newspaper in the parlour; he came out and went into the street - I went into the parlour, saw the candle taken out of the candlestick, and put into a glass, lighted; I followed, and saw him in Marylebone-street - he ran away; I called stop thief! he threw one candlestick down, which was given to me, and he was secured.(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN VANSITTART. I heard a cry of Stop thief! and saw the prisoner throw away a candlestick.
GUILTY. Aged 35. - Confined Six Months.

212. FREDERICK BLACKFORD and WILLIAM BLACKFORD were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of November, 4 bushels of soot, value 4s., the goods of Stephen Wright.
STEPHEN WRIGHT. I am a chimney-sweeper, and live in Stonecutter-alley. I keep my soot in Parker-street;the prisoners are sweeps, but not in my employ, and had no right in my cellar; I had locked it up - I found the lock taken off, and a sack, which did not belong to me, filled with soot, which was taken from my heap.
EDWARD COLT. I am a Policeman. On the 17th of November, about half-past nine o'clock at night, I was going through Parker-street, and saw Wright's cellar open; I called to know who was there - the light was immediately put out; I called three times, and one of the prisoners came up - he said he was picking cinders for his master; I asked who his master was - he said Wright, and that he had left the lock at his master's; I asked who was talking to him - he said there was nobody below; I gave him in custody, and found the other man laying on his face, under an archway.
F. BLACKFORD - GUILTY. Aged 20.
W. BLACKFORD - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Whipped and Discharged.

213. GEORGE BEST was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of October, 2 books, value 14s., the goods of John Pearman.
JAMES MUNTING. I keep a book-shop in the New-cut. On the 27th of October the prisoner came to sell two books for 2s. 6d.; I asked if they belonged to him - he said Yes, and that he lived at Kennington; I offered him 1s. 6d., and sent Nathan with him, to see if he lived there.
NATHAN NATHAN. I went with the prisoner, and when we got three quarters of a mile, I asked if he lived at Queen's-row, Kennington; he said he had lived there three months ago - he at last ran away; I overtook him and gave him in custody.
ALICE STACEY. I am pew-opener at Tavistock chapel, Drury-lane. On the 27th of October I saw a strange man go into Mr. Pearman's pew - it was not the prisoner; but he is something like a man who gave me a note that night for a gentlemen, who, he said, would come to take a sitting.
JOHN PEARMAN. My family attend Tavistock chapel. I left these books there on the 23rd of October; they are mine.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that he was a book-hawker, and had taken the books in exchange for others.
NOT GUILTY.

First London Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
214. EDWARD ANDERSON was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of William Rultish Manger, from his pocket.
WILLIAM RULTISH MANGER. I am a town-traveller, and live in Salter-street, Cannon-street-road. On the 25th of October, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, I was about half-way down Fleet-street, on the pavement, and felt my handkerchief go from my coat pocket; I put my hand down, turned round immediately, and saw it laying on the ground, and the prisoner in the officer's hands - I had used it as I came through Temple-bar.
THOMAS LIGHTFOOT. I am a street-keeper. I watched the prisoner for some time, going up and down Fleet-street, with another; they followed another gentleman, who went into Serjeant's-inn - they then followed Mr. Manger; I saw the prisoner put his hand into Mr. Manger's pocket, and draw the handkerchief out - I took hold of the end of it in his hand, as he was handing it over to his companion; part of it went on the ground - his companion ran away; I should know him again - he was there ready to receive it; I did not see Mr. Manger at the moment, but I called out; he turned round, and claimed the handkerchief - the other was a youth, stouter and taller than himself; the prisoner said he had no parents, and did it for want - he had no money.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was going by, the other boy took it out, and chucked it on my shoulder.
GUILTY. Aged 17 - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18311201-215]
215. JOHN GURREN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of December, 1 box, value 6d.; 1 shawl, value 6d.; 2 frocks, value 6d.; 4 pinafores, value 4s.; 1 pair of shoes value 2s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 1s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 6d.; 1 tippet, value 6d., and 1 1/2 yard of ribbon, value 6d., the goods of Edmond Bird.
EDMOND BIRD. I keep the George, West-street, Smithfield. Last Thursday night, about six o'clock, a bandbox, containing these articles was put into a butcher's cart at my door, for my nephew to take with my daughter to South-street, Park-lane - the cart had not left the door ten minutes before I heard the box was stolen; my nephew is not here - he lives in the country; the prisoner was brought to my house in about half an hour, with the box, in the same state as before.
WILLIAM BUSHNELL. I am a basket-maker, and live at the Crown, in West-street, Bird sent for me to mind the cart, which stood at his door; I saw his little girl and the box put in - there was a tail to the cart; it left the door a little after six o'clock - I went into the tap-room to finish a pint of beer, and heard the cart come back very quick Bird's nephew said the box was gone - Bird called me; I went out, and found the prisoner in Black Boy-alley, West-street, with another person, who had got the box; I passed them, and then returned back quick - they both made a full stop together; I said that was the box I was looking for, that it belonged to Mr. Bird - the prisoner then laid hold of it; I stopped and said, "I must have it" - the prisoner said, "No, let it be," and "I will put you in it;" it is a lonely place - I did not like to stop him; I took it from them, and told the prisoner I would not say who had possession of it; he gave it to me with great reluctance - the other never spoke; Bird insisted on knowing who I got it from, and I told him - I knew him before for some years; he jobbed for people.
THOMAS STALIAN. I am a patrol of St. Sepulchre's. I apprehended the prisoner about half-past seven o'clock on Thursday evening, in King-street, Snow-hill; he had another person with him - I said I had information about him; he said I was deceived in my information - I said if so it would be better for him; I had received information of the cart being robbed, but all I told him was, that I had received information about him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming from my uncle's about half-past six o'clock, and met this young man in the alley, with the box; I knew him, and asked how he did - the witness came up, and took the box out of his hand.
GUILTY. Aged 21 - Transported for Seven Years.

217. ELIZA BULLARD was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of October, 2 shifts, value 3s., the goods of Ann Alderton.
ANN ALDERTON. I lodge at Paddington, and am a laundress; the prisoner worked for me. On the 13th of October I was going to send these shifts home, and missed them - I asked if she knew anything of them; she said I could not have had them - I am answerable for them.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not sent me to pawn a sheet? A. Yes, one of my own; I never sent you to pawn customers' linen.
WILLIAM LANGFORD. I am shopman to Mr. Till, a pawnbroker, in Edgware-road. On the 13th of October the prisoner pawned this shift for 1s., in her own name.
WILLIAM PADDINGTON. I am a shopman to a pawnbroker in Lisson-grove. On the 14th of October a shift was pawned for 1s., in the name of Eliza Bullard; I am not positive whether it was the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 30.

218. ELIZA BULLARD was again indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of July, 1 bed, value 20s.; 2 pillows, value 5s.; 1 bolster, value 1s.; 2 sheets, value 4s.; 2 pillow-cases, value 1s.; 1 quilt, value 2s.; 2 blankets, value 4s.; 1 carpet, value 1s.; 1 looking-glass, value 2s.; 1 teapot, value 1s., and 1 candlestick, value 1s., the goods of Catherine Davies.
CATHERINE DAVIES. I am a widow, of Earl-street, Lisson-grove. The prisoner rented my front room, second floor, furnished, for six weeks, and left about the 20th of October, without notice; I received a letter, in consequence of which, I examined her room, and missed these articles - I found the duplicates of some on her chimney-piece.
WILLIAM LANGFORD. I am a shopman to a pawnbroker. I have a blanket, sheet, pillow, carpet, and looking-glass, pawned at different times, in the name of Bullard, from the 27th of July to the 13th of September; I took most of them in - I cannot swear the prisoner pawned them, but to the best of my belief, it was her pawned the carpet - I have seen her at our shop; five of these duplicates correspond with the articles.
WILLIAM PADDINGTON. I have a tea-pot and napkin, pawned on the 8th of August, by the prisoner, in the name of Payne - here are the duplicates of them.
THOMAS TRINGHAM. I received the prisoner in charge, from Mrs. Davies, who gave me thirteen duplicates.
Prisoner. I intended to redeem them.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Seven Years.

HENRY GARNETT. I am a Policeman. I received the prisoner in charge; he said it was his first offence, and asked forgiveness - a woman threw the coat on his arm, and ran away.(Property produced and sworn to.)

FREDERICK EVANS. I saw the prisoner stand up, and take the pork off the hook - he put it behind him.

221. JANE WHITE, SARAH BRAY, and MARY TAYLER were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of November, 1 tea-caddy, value 4s., and 1 cap, value 1s., the goods of Joshua Walden.
HARRIET WALDEN. I am the wife of Joshua Walden, of Goswell-road, a carcase-butcher. On the 5th of November I was in the first floor, and saw the three prisoners separately leave the passage of our house, about half-past eleven o'clock in the morning; they were all strangers - the street door was left open; I immediately suspected they had been in the back parlour, where this caddy and cap were three minutes before - I came down, and they were gone; I followed the prisoners to the first turning, and there observed them looking at something - Tayler and White immediately turned up Goswell-street; Bray went up Wynyatt-street - I called Wright, who stopped her, and I saw her deliver him the caddy and cap.
THOMAS WRIGHT. I live in Rosamond-street. I secured Bray by Mrs. Walden's desire, and took the caddy and two other parcels from her; she dropped the cap, which Mrs. Walden claimed.
WILLIAM KING. I am a Policeman. I received the prisoner in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Bray's Defence. I met Tayler, who said she was going after a situation; I went with her to a bonnet-shop; a boy asked her to hold the caddy and cap - she gave it to me to go and look after the boy, as she did not think it was honestly got.
Tayler's Defence. I met Bray, and went with her - we met White in Noble-street; we went to the bonnet-shop - a boy came, and asked us to hold these things a moment; he did not return - I gave them to Bray, and went to look for him.
WHITE - GUILTY. Aged 16.
BRAY - GUILTY. Aged 17.
TAYLER - GUILTY. Aged 18.
[trialAccount t18311201-222]
222. JANE WHITE, SARAH BRAY, and MARY TAYLER were again indicted for stealing, on the 5th of November, 5 yards of muslin, value 3s., the goods of Sarah Withrington.
MARY ANN JOHNSON. I am in the employ of Sarah Withrington, who keeps a child-bed linen warehouse, in Myddleton-street, about ten minutes' walk from Walden's. On the 5th of November, about eleven o'clock in the morning, the three prisoners came into the shop together, to look at a cap and some lace; I took a cap from the window, to show them - they bought nothing, and all left together; they did not ask for muslin, but this piece was on the counter, within their reach, in a brown paper parcel; I missed it about twenty-five minutes after they left - nobody else had been in; I went to look after them, and saw them in custody in about half an hour, with the muslin divided into two parcels - it was all in one when they took it.
MRS. WALDEN. When Wright stopped Bray she gave him two parcels, which Johnson claimed.
WILLIAM WRIGHT. Bray delivered me two parcels, which Johnson claimed.(Property produced and sworn to.)
WILLIAM KING. I found one parcel on White, and the other was produced.
The prisoners were all convicted. - Transported for 7 Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-223]
223. WILLIAM TAYLOR and WILLIAM EVANS were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of November, 3 silver watch-cases, value 3l., the goods of Edward Keat, their master.
EDWARD KEAT. I am a watchmaker, and live in Banner-street. St. Luke's - the prisoners were in my employ. On the 19th of November I missed five watch-cases, which should have been in Taylor's hands, to be worked on in my shop; I asked Taylor, in Evans' presence, what had become of them - he told me they were not properly sprung, that the bowls were loose, and they were returned to Mr. Allen, from whom they had been received; I went to Allen, and not finding them, I returned - I told Taylor they were not at Allen's, and asked what had become of them; he then said he had given them to Evans to take home to his wife at dinner time, to instruct her to give them to Allen - Evans said he took them home, and was surprised his wife had not delivered them; I then went to Evans' house - they were not found there: I then had the prisoners taken into custody, and, in my presence, was found on Evans, two duplicates; I went to the pawnbroker's, and found one case, which I knew, and have since seen two others in the hands of Mr. Moss, which are mine; my wife also gave me two duplicates.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Taylor is the person in whose custody they should be? A. Yes; he is my foreman - it was his duty to examine the eases: I send them back if not perfect - workmen do not take work home without permission at any time; I had seen them in Taylor's possession - he keeps them in a box in the shop, of which he has the key, and at night the boxes are put into the iron chest.
Cross-examined by MR. ROE. Q.Have you a large shop? A. No - I had only the prisoners at that time in my employ; Evans is a finisher of movements, and had nothing to do with cases; he has had work home to finish.
SOPHIA KEAT. I am the prosecutor's wife. On the 19th of November I went to Evans' house, to know if the cases were there; Mrs. Evans produced two duplicates to me - I gave them to my husband: I went to Taylor's house on Sunday morning, the 20th, and a lodger delivered me a silver case; Mrs. Taylor brought three more that morning, and in the evening I received three more.
EDWARD KEAT. These are not the cases alluded to in this indictment.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Are not the cases included in the indictment one, the duplicate of which was found on Evans, and the other two which your wife got from Evans' wife? A. Yes.
WILLIAM KEAT. I am the prosecutor's son. I know the watch-cases to be my father's.
JAMES HALLAM. I am a watch-springer and liner - I work for Mr. Keat. I had no cases sent back to me by Taylor or Evans as imperfect - I saw three before the Magistrate, and knew them to be the cases I had sent to Mr. Keat.
JOHN CLARK. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Old-street. I have a silver watch-case, pawned on the 17th of November, in the name of John Brown, No. 42, Radnor-street, for 10s. - I do not know who by.
ROBERT MOSS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Goswell-road. I have two pairs of cases, pawned on the 18th of November, for 10s. 6d. each, by a woman, in the name of Ann Brown, No. 2, Ashby-street.
MILES HANDLEY. I am a Policeman. I apprehended the prisoners; I found on Evans two duplicates for watch-cases, pawned on the 17th and 18th of November, in the name of Brown, and one for the case, pawned at Clark's; Mr. Keat delivered two duplicates belonging to Moss' shop to me.
JOHN CLARK. This is the duplicate of the transaction of the 17th of November.
ROBERT MOSS. These are the duplicates I gave the person.(Property produced and sworn to.)
TAYLOR - GUILTY. Aged 31.
EVANS - GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-224]
224. WILLIAM SHEPHERD was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of November, 1 pair of boots, value 10s., the goods of Thomas Cogswell.
THOMAS COGSWELL. I am a shoemaker, and live at New Brentford. These boots hung at my door; I missed them about four o'clock - they were brought back to my shop in about ten minutes.
THOMAS HILL. I am in Cogswell's service. I saw the prisoner take these boots, and run away with them - I went after him; I lost sight of him for about half an hour, and then saw him in custody - he had not got the boots then; I knew him well before - he lived at Old Brentford.
Cross-examined by MR. SMITH. Q. Did he stand some time at the shop before he took them? A. He stood about five minutes looking at the window: I have known him twelve months, but do not suppose he knew me - the boots hung outside the shop; they were brought back by somebody. (Property produced and sworn to)
Prisoner's Defence. The witness said before the Justice, that his next door neighbour called out that somebody had run away with the shoes, not that he saw me take them.
THOMAS HILL. I saw him run away with them.
WILLIAM SHEPHERD. I am the prisoner's father. I went to the one Tun public-house, about two o'clock on this afternoon, and left about ten minutes to five - my son was not out of my sight more than ten minutes of that time - that was about four o'clock; this was about a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's.
COURT. Q. Did you see him after four o'clock? A. Yes; he returned, and drank with me - I told the Magistrate of this.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Confined One Month and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18311201-225]
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
225. JOHN LAWRENCE was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of November, 1 copper, value 9s., the goods of John Dawson.
JAMES CHAMBERS. I am a Policeman. On the evening of the 8th of November I was in the City-road, and saw the prisoner with something in a bag; I asked him what it was - he said a copper, which he had bought of a broker in Old-street-road; I took him to Mr. Dawson's shop, and while I went in to ask if he bought it there, he sat it down, and ran away - I followed, and took him.
JOHN DAWSON. I live in Old-street-road, and am a broker. This copper was taken from outside my door.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that a man had taken him to the shop, and employed him to carry the copper, representing that he had purchased it.
GUILTY. Aged 54.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-226]
226. JOHN LIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of November, 8 half-crowns, 20 shillings, and 4 sixpences, the monies of Edward Spooner, his master.
EDWARD SPOONER. I lived in Marchmont-street, Westminster - the prisoner was in my service. On the 7th of November I gave my wife two guineas, to give him to purchase bacon; he did not return, but was apprehended on the 15th; he went to buy the bacon at a warehouse in Tooley-street - the persons from the shop are not here; he brought no bacon to me - I did not desire him to go to a particular shop, but expected he would go there- when apprehended he said he went to a wrestlingmatch, took off his coat and waistcoat, and a fellow ran away with his waistcoat, with 2l. 10s. in it.
ELIZABETH SPOONER. By direction of my husband I gave the prisoner 2l. 2s. in silver - it was in half-crowns, shillings, and sixpences; I told him it was to buy bacon- he said, "Yes, I know that;" he never brought any -I have not seen him since till to-night.
Prisoner. Master owed me ten weeks' wages, and gave me 10s. to pay as earnest for the bacon. Witness. I gave you the money, and you showed me a handful of silver besides; I think he had run more in debt than my husband owed him - he used to sleep at our house.
THOMAS GIBLET. On the 10th of November the prisoner came to my house - he said he had been to a wrestling-match, and taken off his waistcoat, but as luck would have it, he had taken his money out of that pocket, and put it into his breeches pocket, and they did not rob him; he pulled out a handful of half-crowns, shillings, and sixpences, to the amount of 2l. or 3l. - I keep an ale-shop in Union-street; he is in the habit of coming and leaving meat at my house - he paid for what he had in silver.
Prisoner. I had the money from him to pay for the beer. Witness. I paid him 8s. 5d. that night on his master's account, not knowing he had left, but he paid for his beer before that; he said he was going to pay 2l. or 3l. for carriage - I am certain he paid me before I paid him any money.
JOHN WATERS. I am a weaver. I saw the prisoner at Giblet's - he said he had been wrestling with a man at Hackney; that he put his coat and waistcoat on the taproom table, went out for a certain purpose, and when he returned the waistcoat was gone, but as luck would have it, he had put his money from his waistcoat pocket into his trousers, and I heard the money jink; he drank part of two pots of beer with me.
BENJAMIN BARNARD. I apprehended the prisoner on the 15th of November.
Prisoner's Defence. Master gave me 5s. as earnest for the bacon, and told me to go to Hackney, with Palmer, to look for a shop for him to answer his purpose better than this one; I was obliged to give Plamer victuals, as he would not be out all day without any - he told me often, if Palmer wanted money to let him have it; I bought some pork, which I sold, and gave master the profit - when I offered him money, he said, "Keep it in your pocket;" on the Sunday we reckoned up and every thing was right, and on Monday he gave me money to buy a pair of shoes, which I wanted.
EDWARD SPOONER. The 7th of November was on Monday; I gave him no money to buy shoes - I knew he had money in his pocket, and told him to buy a pair; that was in addition to the two guineas - I may owe him 4s. or 5s.; he had other money of mine, which he never returned to me.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-227]
227. PATRICK CORMACK was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of November, 4 flag-stones, value 2s., the goods of James Mitchell and others.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
JOSEPH KING. I live in New-cut, Lambeth - my father is a baker. On the 29th of November, about five o'clock in the evening, I was in Princes-street, Westminster, and saw the prisoner near the pump, picking up the large flag-stones - he got behind the pump, and lifted them on another man's shoulder; I saw him take three - the other man went round by the Abbey, round a dark corner, and up Dean-street; the prisoner remained on the spot - the man returned, he put another stone on his shoulder, and walked up Dean-street; I was looking on deliberately - I went and told the Policeman - we cameround by Princes-street, and at last went back and saw the prisoner and another man come out of his own house, in Little Dean-street; we lost sight of him - it was about five o'clock in the evening; I knew the prisoner before, and am sure he is the man.
Cross-examined by MR. SMITH. Q.What are you? A. I lived at a baker's about three months ago - I am now looking for a situation; it was rather dark when this happened - I stood at the corner of Tothill-street, and could see which way they went; the man turned up Fludyer-court once, and the next time up Dean-street; I sometimes looked at him and somethimes at the prisoner.
Q. Did you not first charge the prisoner's brother with doing this? A.When he stood up I said it was not him.
DANIEL REARDON. I am a Policeman. King gave me information on the 29th of November, and I took the prisoner in bed with another man; he said he knew nothing about any stones - I asked if he had got any in a court, at the back of his house; he said, "No, I have no stones there;" I said, "Well, this boy says he saw you take some there;" he said, "Oh, they are my stones;" that he had bought them of one person and another - he could not exactly say who; I went with Mr. Howell, and brought the stones from the court to Newway watch-house; King pointed out where they were taken from - they are large flag-stones - we compared them with the place, and four exactly fitted the place; we found fourteen - after he gave me information I went to a court in the Almonry, and found some large flagstones; I watched, and the prisoner and another man came out of the house; King immediately said, "These are the two men;" they went towards the stones - I afterwards went and found them in bed, as I have stated.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he go up to the stones? A. No, because he saw me - I am certain he faced and saw me; they were regular square stones - one in particular fitted in the joint; King at first charged the prisoner's brother with this.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did the prisoner claim the stones, and say he had bought them? A. Yes; I told him there were fourteen; he said he had bought them all - no stone would fit, unless it was the size of the one taken up -King pointed out a small stone to me, which fitted exactly.
JOSEPH KING re-examined. The man only carried one stone at a time - I saw him come three times; the man who came out with the prisoner was not his brother.
GEORGE HOWELL. I am an inspector of the Police. -Reardon's evidence is correct; the prisoner told me he had bought the stones of a man named Sweeney, at 2d. a foot.
The Act of Parliament, vesting the property as James Mitchell 's and others, was put in.
Prisoner's Defence. I have a witness - it is impossible I could pull a stone out of the ground, I was so ill.
EDWARD CORMACK. I am the prisoner's brother. I was charged with taking these stones, but did not - I live at No. 4, Little Dean-street.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You was not at your brother's house that night? A. I was at my own house; we live together.
DANIEL REARDON. I have seen the prisoner about the street with a stick - I believe he is capable of taking a stone up to put on another man's shoulder.
JAMES DOWIE. I know the prisoner has had a paralytic attack, and been in two hospitals; he is paralysed on his right side, and always walked with a stick - I cannot say whether he was incapable of lifting a stone.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18311201-228]

Strongly recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor, having given immediate information to lead to the recovery of the property - he received a good character.

[trialAccount t18311201-229]
229. WILLIAM SYKES was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of September, 22 yards of woollen cloth, value 20l., and 40 yards of kerseymere, value 16l., the goods of Samuel Johnston.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
SAMUEL JOHNSTON. I live in Addle-street. On the 20th of September the prisoner came to my house, and said he wanted a piece of black cloth, and black kerseymere for a cash customer of his westward; I sent them by a porter - the prisoner and I met the porter at Dimsdale's, St. Ann's, chop-house; the goods were delivered to him, in my presence: we dismissed the porter there, and the prisoner took the goods out under his arm - he did not tell me where they were going, but said it was a cash customer in that neighbourhood; he said he was going to sell them for cash to a customer of his; he came back with the goods, and said he had failed in selling them; he proposed as we had dismissed the porter, that he should leave them at Dimsdale's all night - I agreed; I saw him two or three days after, and proposed sending for them - he said I was not known at the house, and they would not deliver them to me; I saw him again in a few days, and said they ought to be sent for - he said he would send for them; I saw him again in a day or two - he told me he had sent Cleasby for them with a written order; on seeing him again, he said he had sent Cleasby with a note for them, but they refused to deliver them, not knowing his hand-writing - I called at Dimdale's the next day but one; I saw the prisoner in the City afterwards, and told him he had told me a falsehood, for Dimsdale had told me they had been gone this ten days or a fortnight, and that he, Sykes, had taken them away - he said I was mistaken, that he had taken them from Dimsdale's, and left them with Burrows, a tailor, for cash: I inquired who Burrows was - he said he was an intimate friend and companion of Peter Thompson 's, who I knew; I said, "If so he must be a highly respectable man" - I saw him again in a day or two afterwards, and urged the necessity of his calling on Burrows, for the cash or telling me where he lived, that I might call myself, and get it - he said Burrows was gone over to Boulogne on an excursion with Mr. Thompson, and would not be back for a week; I at last called at Thompson's, and when I met the prisoner again, I told him Mr. Thompson's man did not seem to know Burrows - he said that was all nonsense,that the foreman did not know all Mr. Thompson's acquaintances; he told me afterwards that Thompson and Burrows had returned, and he would go for the money - he came back, and told me they had slept but one night in town, and had gone out on a shooting party; I at last gave him into custody - I was to have had the money immediately after the 20th of September, but for his representations; Cleasby was taken up by me on another charge, and in his possession was found a duplicate.
COURT. Q. When he returned and said he had not met with the cash customers, why did you not have the goods back? A. He proposed that they should be left at the house, and we should send the porter for them next day, as he was gone; I conceived he was then bound to send them home - if he had sold them for cash the next day, I should not have found any fault.
Q. You would have given him credit for them if he had sold them? A. No; he was my agent only - he was bound to sell only in my name, and with my invoice; that was our general agreement - he never sold on his own account, to my knowledge; I did not furnish him with an invoice, because I expected the goods back - if he had met with a customer he was to make out an invoice in my name, and give a receipt in my name, as my agent.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Do you keep a warehouse in Addle-street? A. It is a warehouse for me and others; a general deposit for two or three different people - Wilkinson keeps the warehouse; I have been there about six months - I took the benefit of the Insolvent Act about eighteen months ago; I seldom have so little as 50l. worth of goods at the warehouse - I dare say I have that amount now, but will not swear it, as I have been so engaged in this business; the prisoner was in my employ- his employment ceased when I discovered this; it commenced about the 10th or 12th of August.
Q. Was there not some dissolution of a connexion between you before? A. I trusted him with some small commissions, and gave him credit for some small sums; I never employed him to pawn goods for me, or to get them pawned - there was a written agreement between us when I dismissed him, which was about the 8th or 10th of November; and after I made a charge against him at Worship-street, I destroyed that agreement, because I dismissed him; he had lost my confidence, and I tore it up in his presence - the charge at Worship-street was about two other cloths; I think that was on the 2nd of November -(looking at three papers) these are my hand-writing; I believe this invoice is all my writing, except the signature of Sykes - here is my own signature; the prisoner came to Worship-street of his own accord one day, and I agreed to let him go, on the 2nd of November, without bail, and he was to come on the Monday - he came again in the meantime, and was detained on another charge; he came one day when I did not attend; I had told him I would not, in consequence of promises - I preferred this charge at the Mansion-house, on the 24th of November, I think; I had my suspicions about this cloth when at Worship-street, but had not seen Thompson himself - I knew a duplicate had been found on the 5th of November, but did not know it was for my cloth; I received the duplicate from Cleasby, about the 8th or 9th of November - 9l. 10s. had been obtained on the cloth: the prisoner was not present when I received the duplicate - I got a duplicate for 10l. from Sykes, about the 8th of November; it was after I had got the first from Cleasby, and after he was at Worship-street- it is dated the 29th of October; I had both these duplicates when I agreed not to appear at Worship-street - I got the one dated in October after he was discharged.
Q. Did you not get that ticket from him the very morning of the day you was to appear against him? A. No, I am certain I did not get it till after I was to appear against him at Worship-street; I will not swear it was not on the same day.
COURT. Q. Who did you receive it from? A.From the prisoner, about the 8th; I did not prefer this charge till the 24th - I had seen him several times between the 8th and 24th, and never took any steps.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you not receive from him, 4l. in money? A. I did after his discharge - I mean after the time I had appeared against him; I received it on the 3rd or 4th; he was discharged on the 7th: I advanced him 10s. 6d. when he was discharged - I do not know that it was to pay his fees; I knew my cloth was gone, but I had not seen Thompson to ascertain if he knew Burrows.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18311201-230]
230. CHARLES KENNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of October, 1 saw, value 1s.; 1 stock and bit, value 2s.; 1 holdfast, value 18d.; 1 bed-wrench, value 6d.; 2 chisels, value 1s., and 1 screw-driver, value 6d., the goods of Thomas Harris.
THOMAS HARRIS. I am a bedstead-maker, and live in Bagnigge-wells-road. The prisoner was my journeyman; he left on the 1st of October, and I missed all these tools - he had no authority to pawn them; I met him on the 21st of October, in Gower-street, and he ran away - I called a Policeman, who took him.
GEORGE CHAPMAN. I am an apprentice to Mr. Nichols, pawnbroker, Gray's Inn-lane. I produce all these tools which were pawned by the prisoner, in three pledges; I am certain of him.
ROBERT TURNER. I am a Policeman. I apprehended the prisoner, and found the duplicates of the tools on him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Confined Three Months.

231. JOHN EVANS was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of November, 9 lbs. of bacon, value 5s., the property of Patrick Neal.
FREDERICK MILES. I am a Policeman. On the 25th of November, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner and another lad in John's-row; the lad went into Neal's shop, took the bacon off the counter, and gave it to the prisoner in the passage - they both ran away; I followed, and took the prisoner with it under his arm.
MARY NEAL. I am the wife of Patrick Neal, of John's-row. I heard the scales rattle in the shop, I ran out, and the bacon was gone; the Policeman brought the prisoner in with it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was not in the passage; a boy betrayed me from work, and put the bacon into my arms.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Week.

232. WILLIAM BARNETT was indicted for stealing on the 22nd of November, 9 locks, value 27s.; 1 pair of brass butts, value 15s.; 2 bolts, value 8s.; 6 ends for blinds, value 6d.; 1 brush, value 6d.; 1 latch, value 2s. 6d.; 4 locks, value 2s.; 6 drawer-handles, value 18d., and 8 window-rods, value 2s., the goods of Hugh Welch Cooper.
BENJAMIN PHILLIPS. I am a Policeman. On the 28th of November, about half-past five o'clock in the morning, I stopped the prisoner in Gray's Inn-lane, carrying a heavy basket - I asked what he had there; he said some furniture to put in a house in Blackfriars-road, and that his father was a master builder; I said I did not believe him - he said I might see what he had got; I examined it, and said I should take him to the watch-house - he took up the basket, walked a few steps, then threw it on me, and ran away; I sprang my rattle, and Haines stopped him in three minutes - I am sure of his person.
WILLIAM HAINES. I am a Policeman. I was in Bell-court, Gray's Inn-lane, and heard a rattle spring - the prisoner passed me almost immediately; I pursued, and never lost sight of him till I brought him back - he refused to go; there was a struggling between us, and he took hold of the lamp-post, but I brought him back.
GEORGE EVA. I am foreman to Hugh Welch Cooper, a builder in Wakefield-street, St. Pancras - these things are all his. On the evening of the 21st, at half-past five o'clock, I put them on a shelf in the first floor of his workshop - the door was locked; I found the window forced in next morning, about seven o'clock, and every thing in confusion, from one end to the other; the premises are near the bottom of Gray's Inn-lane.
Prisoner's Defence. I live by King's-cross - I was going to work, and found these things in the fields.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.

235. WILLIAM FARROW was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of March, 1900 sheets of printed paper, value 12l., the goods of George Smallfield, his master.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
GEORGE SMALLFIELD, I am a printer, and live at Homerton; the prisoner was in my service for sixteen years and a half. In consequence of having paper burnt, I had occasion lately to take stock; I missed a great number of volumes - I never gave the prisoner permission to take these volumes, and sell them for waste-paper; they are all saleable works - the Rev. Mr. Aspland's hymn forms a part of them, which is a work now on sale.
COURT. Q. Did you ever give him authority to sell waste-paper? A. Not on his own account; he had sold some for me, but certainly he had no authority to sell this - this is not waste-paper.
Cross-examined by MR. ROWE. Q. Had you given him permission to sell any other volumes? A.None for himself, he has on my account; I took stock in October last - my business is not very extensive; I have about twelve workmen now - they could go to my warehouse, but the greater part of them had no right to go; no other person has ever sold paper for me.
ALFRED ANDREWS. I am in the prosecutor's employ. About the 4th of November, 1830, I was returning from Stamford-hill, and saw the prisoner in the high road, going to London, with master's cart; I got up in the cart - there were two bundles of paper in it - he went to Old-street-road, and left the paper at Riley's house; the prisoner gave me 6d. - he had before that spoken to me respecting some paper which I saw him take; he held up his finger, and said, "Never you say a word, or you will ruin me and my family;" that was one day when we took some paper - that was all that passed.
COURT. Q. A word about what? A. About some paper that was in the cart, I suppose; he took some six or seven times - I do not know when he said that; it was some time before, when we were taking paper there.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been in your master's service? A.Three years next May; I do not know where this paper came from - the first time I saw it was at the top of my master's lane; I had before been with him when he took paper - I did not mention this to my master till after the prisoner was in trouble.
JOHN RILEY. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Old-street. Shortly before Christmas, 1830, the prisoner brought some printed paper to me, in Mr. Smallfield's cart, which I bought of him; it was, I think, between 30 lbs. and 40 lbs. - I sold that paper, with others, to Faulkner, Mr. Wilson's man; the prisoner did not make any representation to me - it was in two bundles, and I paid him 3d. per 1b. for it; I sold some of the best at 4 1/2d. per 1b., and used the inferior part at my own place.
Cross-examined. Q. At what hour of the day was this? A. He generally came between ten and one o'clock- I saw his master's name on the cart, and he brought it openly into the shop.
WILLIAM FAULKNER. I am servant to Mr. Gilbert Wilson - he lives in Upper Thames-street, and deals in waste-paper. I bought some paper of Mr. Riley the latter end of March, or the beginning of April; I took it home to my master's - I remember Mr. Smallfield coming and seeing the same paper which I got from Riley - this is it; I am sure it is part of what I bought of Mr. Riley.
Cross-examined. Q.What is your master? A. A rag and waste-paper dealer; we buy a great deal from various quarters - we frequently buy volumes of booksellers; I took this home, and it remained there six or seven weeks; I took other paper with this, but of a different size - this is of a particular size; I think this paper remained at my master's from March to October; there is another man who weighs the paper, and stows it away in piles.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you quite sure this is some ofthe paper you got from Mr. Riley's? A. I am quite confident of it.
MR. SMALLFIELD. I have no hesitation in saying that this is the property of the Rev. Mr. Aspland, intrusted to my care - I never authorized the prisoner to sell it; it is gathered into volumes - these are half volumes.
Cross-examined. Q.Can you possibly identify this paper as coming out of your possession? A.Certainly; I have the work in this state for sale, and send it in this state to different booksellers, when I have orders for it - there is a running account between me and the prisoner - I cannot state the balance, but there was never any running of the paper sold by him; he always brought me the money for that; the account was only 1s. a we he requested me to keep for him against his - I do not often send out copies of books which are out of point, not oftener than once in two or three years; I have not taken stock of some of my articles for three or four years, but I think I have of this work.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you ever give him permission to sell Mr. Aspland's hymns? A. No, never.
COURT. Q. Did you ever give him liberty to take paper and sell it without showing it to you? A. Never- this had not been considered as waste-paper; this had been in my possession ever since the prisoner has been with me; it is not much in demand, as there are two cheaper editons - this had lain by, and I was less likely to have my attention called to it.
The prisoner received an excellent character.
GUILTY. Aged 51.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-236]
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
236. JOHN VELLELEY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of November, 21 lbs. of brass, value 5s., the goods of George Green and others, his masters.
WILLIAM JUDGE. I am a Thames Police-officer. I met a woman in the Commercial-road, on the morning of the 17th of November, carrying something very heavy under her cloak; I stopped her, and found it was ingots of brass - I went to Mr. Green's yard, and saw the prisoner standing outside the gate; he asked me and my brother officer who was wanted: I said, " John Velleley " - he then begged of me not to let the firm know; I said,"We must:" I heard him tell my brother officer, that he got the metal out of the yard, and he acknowledged at the office, that the woman was his wife.
THOMAS GRAY. I was with Judge, and saw the prisoner near the prosecutor's yard; I asked where he got the metal which his wife was carrying - he said he got it from Mr. Wigram's yard, and gave it to his wife to get a trifle.
NATHANIEL CATON. I am in the employ of George Green, Wigram, and Green - the prisoner was nearly four years in their employ, as a journeyman: I cannot swear to the brass, but I believe it to be theirs - I have fitted these ingots to the moulds, and they fit exactly.
Prisoner. I am sorry for it, they have been good masters to me - I have a wife, and two children.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-237]
237. MARY WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of November, 1 half-crown, the money of William Jackman, from his person.
WILLIAM JACKMAN. I am a sawyer. On the 27th of November, I was walking up Oxford-street with some friends, about one o'clock in the morning; I had had a glass or two of liquor, but I was not intoxicated - the prisoner spoke to us, said she was very cold, and asked us to give her something to drink; we went to the Three Tuns, and had three half-quarterns of gin, and two pints of porter - we went out, and the two young men who had been with me went away; I went up a mews with the prisoner - I felt her hand in my left-hand pocket, where I had a shilling and a half-crown; I felt my pocket, and the half-crown was gone - I said, "You have taken my half-crown;" she denied it; the Policeman came up, and took her - she said at the office that she had one half-crown, but it was her own.
Prisoner. He asked the other men to pay for the gin and beer, and said he had no money. Witness. Yes, I did, but I had put this money by to pay away.
JAMES BARTON. I am a Policeman. The prosecutor charged the prisoner with robbing him; he was quite sober; the prisoner said she had a half-crown belonging to herself, and she gave it up at the watch-house from her pocket, she had no more money about her.
Prisoner's Defence. He accused me of robbing him, and held my two hands; I said, "What are you going to do?" he felt his left-hand pocket, and said, "You have robbed me of half a crown;" I said, I had not; I thought he was going to strike me, and called the Policeman myself - it is my first offence.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Fourteen Years.

JAMES ROWE. I keep a sale-shop, at Kensington. I took the prisoner in out of charity till he got a situation; we parted on the 5th of November - I missed my shoes, and found this pair on him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe his parents are in great distress? A. I went into their house that evening, they did not seem so; I know these shoes are mine.

[trialAccount t18311201-239]
239. OBADIAH WOODCOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of October, 12 ounces of silk, value 1l. 4s., and 9 wooden bobbins, value 9d., the goods of George Devereux Ford.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
MARY ANN MACREE. I am the daughter of George Macree; he lives in Great George-street, and is a weaver. On the 24th of October I was standing at our door; I saw the prisoner another man, and two little boys were looking at their window; the prisoner went by and stood at the prosecutor's door, talking to another man, and the little boy looked out, and said, "Is that you father?" the little boy ran down stairs, and they asked him whether his name was Ford; he said Yes - they said "Where is your father?" he said out; they said, "Is he gone to the public-house?" he said he did not know - they asked if he was gone to the watch-house; he said he did not know - theytook him as far as the corner of the street; the other man then went up stairs with the boy - the prisoner staid at the door about a minute, and then he went in and shut the door; the prisoner went up stairs; I heard the hobbins fall on the floor - the little boy screamed out murder, and said, "Pray don't, I shall be murdered when my father comes home" - I called my father down, and said there were two men robbing Mr. Ford; he ran down, and saw the other man run away - he ran, but could not catch him; I saw the prisoner come out, and cried out Stop thief! and the patrol stopped him six or seven doors from the prosecutor's house.
ROBERT FORD. I am the prosecutor's son. I saw the prisoner and another man come into my father's room, one Monday night; I was looking out at my father's window, and said, "Is that you father?" the prisoner said, "I am not your father, but I want to speak to you;" I went as far as the end of the street, and then I said, "I must not go any farther;" we came back, and they both went up stairs; the other man pretended to be looking at my cap, and he went behind my father's shelf, and filled his pockets with shute; the prisoner then came up - they whispered together, and this prisoner put the bobbins in his hat, with silk on them - then he took a handkerchief out of his pocket, put shute into it, and tied them up; I said, "Oh, don't take my father's shute, I shall get murdered" - he said, "The more you halloo, the more I'll take;" I said, "Murder! thieves! thieves! murder! they have got my father shute;" they then run away - when the prisoner had got two or three doors off, I saw the officer take him; the bobbins were found at the foot of the stairs.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you lose sight of me? A. No; when you ran down stairs I looked out of window and saw you - I am sure he is the man that took my father's shute.
GEORGE DEVEREUX FORD. I am the witness' father. I went out on the 24th of November, but I left my work safe, and when I came home I missed nine bobbins of shute; I found three of them at the foot of the stairs, at the bottom of the passage - the other six are lost; they are worth 24s. or 25s. - if the thief had not been found I should have lost my character for ever.
COURT. Q. Did you know the prisoner before? A. I never knew him before.
Prisoner's Defence. I had a pint or two of beer at the corner of the said street, and as I was going past I heard the two boys call out; I went into the entry to hear what was the matter, and a man rushed out in his shirt sleeves, he took me, and said, "You have been at the work;" I said, "You are mistaken;" he went to get his clothes on, and the patrol took me.
BENJAMIN CRISPIN. I took hold of him at the foot of the stairs, and he dropped the bundle where it was found.
GUILTY. Aged 42. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-240]
240. JAMES YOUNG was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of November, 1 handkerchief, value 5s. the goods of Charles Fentum, from his person.
CHARLES FENTUM. On the 9th of November I was on Westminster-bridge - I did not feel any thing taken, but the officer spoke to me; I missed my handkerchief, which the officer had.
JAMES WILD. I am a Police-officer. I had watched the prisoner for nearly half an hour - there were one or two more with him; they were trying several gentlemen's pockets - I saw the prisoner take this handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket; I seized him, and took it from him, the other got away.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me take it from the prosecutor's pocket? A. Yes, and I took it from your waistcoat.(Property produced and Sworn to.)
