241. ROBERT BUTLER was indicted for bigamy.
MARY ANN ELDRIDGE. I know the prisoner - he married my sister Elizabeth Eldridge; she is alive, and is here; I was present at their marriage, at St. George's church in the Borough, on the 17th of April, 1820.
SAMUEL PRENDERGAST. I am an officer of Lambeth-street. I produce the certificate of the first marriage - I examined it with the book, and found it correct - (read); I took the prisoner in Gravel-lane.
JOHN PAUL. I am clerk of All Souls', St. Marylebone, and have the register of marriages. On the 28th of September, 1825. Robert Butler, batchelor, and Elizabeth Swinford, widow, were married in that church - I believe the prisoner to be the man.
MARIA CORNISH. I know Elizabeth Swinford; I was in the church, and saw them married - the prisoner is the man; I did not see them again till I saw them over the water - I had known Elizabeth Swinford for some time - her first husband's name was Joseph Swinford.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Had you known the prisoner before? A. Yes, between eleven and twelve years.
COURT TO MARY ANN ELDRIDGE. Q. Did you know any thing of the second wife? A.Sir, I did not.
Cross-examined. Q.Had you been in the daily habit of seeing the prisoner after his marriage with your sister? A. Yes; my sister had a child in about eleven months; they did not live together above two years - a man named Cain has been acquainted with my sister; I have seen them together - I have not visited them: they have visited me; I do not know that my sister ran away to live with Cain, and that Cain came and thrashed the prisoner to make him keep the child - nor that my sister was taken before a Magistrate by the prisoner for ill-treatring him - or that the prisoner was turned out of his house, and all his working-tools taken from him by Cain, at the instigation of my sister - I have seen children at Cain's; I do not know that they were my sister's but they passed as such.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 1 Month.

242. JEREMIAH SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of November, 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d. the goods of Thomas Hill, from his person.
THOMAS HILL. I am a sail-maker. On the 11th of November I was in Ratcliff-highway, between six and seven o'clock at night; I felt something at my right side - I turned, and saw the prisoner; he put his face close to me, and said, "What do you want with me?" I said Nothing - he repeated it again and I said Nothing; I then felt my pocket, and missed my handkerchief; Isaid, "I have lost my handkerchief;" he said, "I saw two urchin thieves on the other side of the way;" he walked with me a little way, till we came to a light; I saw a bulk in front of him, and my handkerchief was hanging down - he walked on to a turning which was no thoroughfare; he turned to go down - I seized him, and cried Stop thief! he struck me - he had a black handkerchief in his hand; I took my handkerchief from under his coat.
Cross-examined by MR. STURGEON. Q. Did he not ask whether you had lost a handkerchief? No - he was intoxicated, but could walk very well.
JOHN EASTERBROOK. I am the officer. I saw the prisoner with this black handkerchief in his hand, and the prosecutor gave me this other.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence I was going to see a friend, and I saw this handkerchief on the ground - I took it up, and walked on; when I came to this street I turned, and the gentleman came up to me; I said, "What do you want?" he saw this handkerchief hang down - he caught hold of it; I took it out, and gave it to him.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Seven Years.

243. WILLIAM TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of October, 7 knives, value 15s., the goods of William Day, his master.
FREDERICK WILSON MILNER. I am a Policeman. On the 23rd of October I saw the prisoner in the Strand, showing some new knives to another boy, and having seen him before in bad company, I took him; he said his father made the knives, and sent him out to sell them - he begged hard of me to go to his father with him, to convince me that what he said was true; I went with him, and as soon as he got to the house he ran up stairs as fast as he could - I heard him say, "Mother - knives - say you gave them to me;" I went up, and he then said he found them in Covent-garden-market - I took him to the watch-house, and found these seven knives on him.
WILLIAM DAY. I am a cutler, and live in the Strand. The prisoner has been in my service about three months - he slept in the shop, and had access to all my drawers; I had sent him on an errand about a week before he was taken, and he never returned; these are my knives - I have some others in my pocket; they correspond exactly.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Do you swear to them by the maker's name? A. I gave an express order to a man to make these particular knives for me, and they have the stamp on them - I swear they had not been sold.
Prisoner's Defence. I never said such a word to my mother.
Two witnesses gave the prisoner a good character, one of whom stated that he would take him as an apprentice.
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Whipped and Discharged.

Prisoner. It was the other boy took it; he gave it to me, and then ran away - I told the officer so.

JOSEPH WILLIAMS. I was standing at my door, and saw the prisoner being taken to the watch-house; I saw him throw away this pencil-case.
JOSEPH FRIANEL CRISTALL. I am a police-officer. I was at the station-house when the prisoner was brought in; I found on him a handkerchief and 1s.(Property produced and sworn to.)

246. JOHN ARUNDEL was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of November, 1 cloak, value 35s, and 1 handkerchief, value 6d., the goods of Susannah Bruce.
JOHN ELLIS. I am a watchman on Westminster-bridge. I met the prisoner on the Middlesex end of the bridge on the 20th of November, after nine o'clock at night; I saw him go and take this bundle out of a four-wheeled chaise, it was behind the driver - he had his right hand on the spring of the chaise as it passed me, and then I saw his left hand draw the bundle out; I took him - the chaise went on, but I advertized the property, and found the owner.
SUSANNAH BRUCE. I am a widow, and live in Alfredplace, Bedford-square; this cloak and handkerchief are mine - I was in the chaise, returning from Vauxhall, about nine o'clock in the evening; the parcel was in the back of the chaise - I did not miss it till I got home; I know no thing of the prisoner.
Prisoner's defence. I saw the bundle in the road, and the watchman came and took me; I never had it in my hand.

247. JAMES WALEY was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of November, 3 loaves of bread, value 1s. 3d., the goods of David Hood.
SAMUEL MOSELEY. I live with Mr. David Hood; he is a baker - I was out with his bread on the 25th of November; I left my basket at the corner of Portman-square for about ten minutes - when I returned, I missed three loaves; I saw the bread and the prisoner in custody - these are the loaves.
HENRY GARNET. I am a Policeman. On the 25th ofNovember, I saw the prisoner on the square side, with these loaves under his arm; when he saw me he ran fast -I went and took him, and asked where he got the bread; he said, "Where did I get it?" as I was taking him along, he threw the loaves into a basket at the corner of Orchard-street, and said, "What is a fellow to do?" he said he had had nothing to eat for two days - he had not a farthing on him, and was nearly naked.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in the greatest distress, and seeing the basket, I took them; I should not have taken three, but they were stuck together.
GUILTY. Aged 24. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-248]
248. EDWARD UPCHURCH and MARY ALBERDINE were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of October, 1 sovereign, the money of John Smith, from his person.
JOHN SMITH. I am a Chelsea pensioner. On the 21st of October I had a sovereign, which was a part of my pension-money; I went to the public-house where I lodge- I was quite capable of taking care of myself; the two prisoners were sitting by me - I took my sovereign out of my pocket; it was in the corner of my handkerchief - I was going to get it changed; Alberdine took it from me - she gave it to Upchurch, and he went away immediately - Alberdine staid a little time, and I argued with her about the money; they had been sitting by my side, but I took no notice of them - I had not known them before; I did not follow Upchurch, because I had a bad leg - I cried out, and threatened Alberdine; I have never seen the sovereign since.
GEORGE FARLEY. I am a labourer, and a pensioner. I was at the public-house where I lodge - I saw Upchurch, and three of his comrades with him; he went out for half an hour, and then returned with a shilling - the prosecutor was drinking there, and Upchurch said, "I will show as much money as you;" the prosecutor said, "If you will show a shilling, I will show a sovereign" - the prosecutor then showed the sovereign from his handkerchief; Alberdine took it and gave it to Upchurch, who started with it- I ran to the bar; the landlord was not at home - I told his daughter; she said, "What can I do? I can't stop him"- Alberdine then got up to go out; the prosecutor held her for a minute or two, but she got away; they were taken the next morning.
Upchurch. Q. Why did not you accuse me of it? A. I did, and tried to stop you; I had heard you say in the morning where you lodged, and I gave information.
WILLIAM BOWEN. I keep the Adam and Eve public-house, not the house where this robbery was committed, but Upchurch came to my house the same afternoon, between three and four o'clock - he changed a half-sovereign, and had two or three pots of beer.
ROBERT DUFF. I am pay-serjeant of the company in which Upchurch is a private in the Scotch Guards. The last money I paid him was 2s. 6d. on the 18th of October - I paid him that for three days, he was under stoppages, and must have been very short of money; I have known him fifteen years in the regiment, his character has been irregular, but I know no dishonesty of him.
HENRY WILLIAMS. I am a constable. I took Alberdine being informed by the landlord of the Three Crowns, that a sovereign had been lost in his house; when I took her, I asked her if she knew any thing about a soldier? she said No - a little boy, who was there, said, "Yes, he lives at the Royal Oak, at Knightsbridge;" I went there, and found he had been absent two days - I afterwards took him in the guard-room.
Upchurch's Defence. I receive 9s. 4d. a week, but I am quartered, and sometimes it does not cost me 2s. a week for living, because I carry out beer for the landlord.
Alberdine put in a written Defence, declaring her innocence.
UPCHURCH - GUILTY. Aged 39.
ALBERDINE - GUILTY. Aged 38.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-249]
249. EDWARD SWEENEY, JAMES HENESSEY, and DENNIS BUCKLEY were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of November, 1 watch, value 25l.; 2 keys, value 3s., and 1 seal, value 5s., the goods of William Hardy, from his person.
WILLIAM HARDY. I am a carpenter. I and my brother were at a beer-shop in Essex-street, Whitechapel, on the 14th of November; we left the house at ten minutes before ten o'clock at night - the prisoners were in the room while I was; there were forty persons in the room -I was quite sober; when I was leaving the room, I put down my hand and felt my watch chain - as I was coming down stairs, Sweeney and Henessey stood one on each side the door; Buckley was a little before them, going down the stairs before me - I do not know that he did any thing to prevent my going down stairs then, but when he came down to the street door, he stopped the doorway up, to prevent my going out - the other two prisoners were then close by me; we at last got out of the house, and I immediately missed my watch - I did not know which way the prisoners had gone - I looked for them, when I missed my watch, and they were all gone; and the people about said it must be these three young men, as nobody else was a thief; I gave information of them - they were all about me, and hustling me at the time Buckley was stopping the doorway.
Cross-examined by MR. STURGEON. Q. I believe this was a smoking-club? A. I believe it is called the Free and Easy club - I do not know whether the prisoners are members, but I saw them in the room; when I got up to go the other persons in the room followed very shortly; no one was near me in going down but the three prisoners - I felt my watch safe while I was in the room, and missed it when I got out at the street door; no one touched me on the stairs but the prisoners - the stairs are narrow; no one attempted to hustle me but the prisoners.
JOHN HARDY. I was with my brother, and saw the three prisoners there; when my brother was going down Henessey and Sweeney got one on each side of the door, and on each side of my brother, and when they got to the end of the passage Buckley stood with his hand across the door, and they were all close to my brother; I had seen the chain of my brother's watch as he left the room, but the seal was out of sight - when we got into the street I asked him if he had lost his watch; he felt, and it was gone - the prisoners were all gone directly.
Cross-examined. Q.This was a club? A. Yes; there were thirty or forty persons in the room - it was the timeto break up; there were some before and some behind us; I was not exactly close to my brother - I cannot say whether there were any other persons exactly on the stairs besides him and the three prisoners; I was behind my brother- there was no one between him and me; I saw him hustled from the top of the stairs to the bottom - some persons might have gone before me.
RICHARD WELSH. I am a labourer. I was at the beershop, and saw the two Hardys go down - I saw Sweeney and Henessey stand by the door; the prosecutor had to go down sideways to pass them; I saw Henessey make a grab at him, but there was another person between me and him, and I could not see what happened; they then shoved the prosecutor down stairs into the street - I saw Henessey put something into his own pocket, and when Hardy said he had lost his watch, I said, "It is Sweeney and Henessey."
Cross-examined. Q.Had you been acquainted with the prisoners before? A. No, I only knew them at that place; I never had any altercation with them, to my recollection - I never was before any Magistrate on a charge of assaulting Sweeney's brother, nor Henessey - my brother was; I never used any threat about Henessey, to my recollection.
HENRY COTTON I am a Police-constable. The three prisoners were taken in company together, in Wentworth-street, about half-past eleven o'clock at night; Hardy gave me a description of them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you find any thing on them? A.About 1l. 0s.7d.; they each had some money - I found no watch.
ABRAHAM SCOTT. I am a Police-serjeant. I heard Sweeney say at the station-house, "The prosecutor cannot say which of the three took his watch."
Sweeney's Defence. Welsh came to me at Lambeth-street Police-office, and asked if I understood Irish - I said Yes; he then asked me to give the watch back - I said I knew nothing about it; he said it was false, for he saw Henessey take it, and give it to me; before the Magistrate he said he saw him take something, but he did not know what.
Henessey's Defence. Welsh went to Sweeney's mother, and said he saw me take the watch, and put it into my pocket: he said he would go to some gin-shop, stay till the row began, and then he would say some other person took it.
Buckley's Defence. The Magistrate asked Welsh if he saw me by the door, and he said he did not see me at all - I was in the house, having a pint of beer.
SWEENEY - GUILTY. Aged 20.
HENESSEY - GUILTY. Aged 20.
BUCKLEY - GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-250]
250. EDWARD TYLER was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of October, 1 watch, value 7l.; 2 seals, value 30s., and 1 watch-key, value 10s., the goods of Henry Vallance, from his person.
HENRY VALLANCE. I was in Chancery-lane on the 31st of October - I had hold of Mr. Nuttall's arm: there had been a fire, and we were looking at one of the engines - there was no crowd at that time; I saw the prisoner before me - there was then a rush - the prisoner came up against me; I felt my watch going, and saw him draw his hand from me; I touched him, and said, "You have got my watch;" he got from me - I went again to take him, and he got between two men; I made a rush, and seized him again - he said, "Search me," but the watch was gone.
Prisoner. Q.Did not I say, "Search me?" A. Yes, you did, the second time, but not the first' just as your body passed me I felt the watch go from me.
HENRY CONSTABLE NOTTALL. I was walking arm-in-arm with the prosecutor; all on a sudden, when one of the engines came up, there was a rush, and we were surrounded - I said to the prisoner, "Let go my arm, or you will hurt it;" he did so, and we got nearly facing each other - a person then brushed past me, and I turned round- Mr. Vallance said, "I have lost my watch;" I saw the prisoner go to two men with his hands closed - the prosecutor touched him, and said, "You have my watch;" he said, "I have not, you may search me;" an officer came up, and he was taken.
CHARLES EVANS. I am a patrol. I was standing watching the gang - there were four or five of them, and they had hustled three or four persons before they bustled the prosecutor; I heard the prosecutor say he had lost his watch, and took the prisoner.
JAMES PALMER. I am a patrol. I was standing by, and heard the prosecutor say he had lost his watch - I took the prisoner, but found nothing on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to see the fire - I was coming towards Fleet-street, and this gentleman said I had taken his watch.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Life.

251. WILLIAM SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of November, 1 hearth-rug, value 4s., the goods of Phillip Dawe.
ELLEN DAWE. I am the wife of Phillip Dawe. This rug was taken off the counter in our shop in Bath-street, on the 24th of November; I was in the parlour - the officer brought it back in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour.
THOMAS BLAIR. I am a Police-constable. I saw the prisoner in Old-street, about thirty or forty yards from the prosecutor's carrying this rug in this bag; I received information from a carman, and took him into custody - he said he had got a rug, which he brought from his house at Pentonville, and was going to his uncle's in Blackfriars-road.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you sure he said he got it from home? A. Yes.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was going down old-street, and turned down Whitecross-street - two men walked before me, and dropped this - I took it up.

252. SARAH SMITH was indicted for stealing on the 26th of November, 1 shirt, value 5s., the goods of William Goodburn.
HENRY NICKS. I am in the service of Mr. William Goodburn - he lives in Union-street, Spitalfields, and is a pawnbroker. The prisoner came there on the 26th of November, to look at some shirts; I showed her some - she bought one, and went away; she came back in about anhour, and said it did not fit; I showed her some more, and while I was serving her I saw her take one from the counter, and conceal it under her shawl; she then asked if we had any children's things - I said Yes; she looked at some but said they did not suit her; she was then going out - I told her she had something: she said she had not - I brought her back, and took it from her.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you known her before? A. Yes, as a customer; the shop was not particularly dark - this could not have happened by accident; it was under her arm, not in her lap - I believe she has things in pawn with us now.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Fined 1s. and Discharged.

253. JOHN STEWART and WILLIAM JEFFERSON were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of November, 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of Joseph Davison, from his person.
JOSEPH DAVISON. On the 12th of November I was in Regent-street, between nine and ten o'clock in the evening- I did not feel any thing taken, but I heard a noise; I turned, and saw the two prisoners laying down on the ground near me; the officer had knocked them down, and had my handkerchief in his hand.
THOMAS HOBBS. I was on duty in Regent-street; I saw the two prisoners and another person with them - I had watched them, and seen them attempting several gentlemen's pockets; I said to my brother officer, "We will follow;" they attempted the prosecutor's pocket several times - Stewart took the handkerchief quite out; the other two were behind him, trying to hide him - I run them all up, knocked them all down, and fell upon them; I secured the two prisoners, and got the cap of the third one, but he escaped.
Jefferson's Defence. It is false; I never was in Stewart's company till we were taken.
STEWART - GUILTY. Aged 16.
JEFFERSON - GUILTY. Aged 17.

254. JOHN SHEPPARD was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of October, 1 trunk, value 7s.; 6 frocks, value 4s.; 8 shirts, value 5s.; 3 yards of lace, value 3s.; 2 aprons, value 9d.; 1 waistcoat, value 1s.; 2 night-caps, value 6d.; 1 pair of trousers, value 1s., and 9 pairs of stockings, value 6s., the goods of James Goulden.
JAMES GOULDEN. I was removing to Petter-street, Westminster, where my wife had taken a room; she had employed the prisoner to remove my things, and I saw him removing some.
JANE GOULDEN. My husband and I were removing to No. 37, Peter-street, on the 19th of September; one of my lodgers told me the prisoner would carry my trunk and other things for me; I employed him to take them - he carried them on his shoulder, and was to take them to the room: I gave him a clock and white hair trunk to carry, and 6d. for it; it contained the articles stated - he went twice; he never returned the second time, and I never saw him till he was taken on Monday, the 19th of November, when he passed by our house; I have not seen my property since.
SARAH ELLIOTT. I live at No. 37, Peter-street. - The prisoner brought a trunk to the house, and put it down before the counter in my shop; I told him to take it up stairs to the room Mrs. Goulden had taken; he said he could not take it up, for he was in a hurry to get some more things; he then went away, and returned in ten minutes, with a man in a green coat, and pointing to the trunk, he said, "There is the trunk;" the other man said it had come to the wrong place - I said he should not take it away, if any one took it it should be the prisoner; the prisoner said, "Well mistress, rather than have any words I will take it;" he took it, and I saw it no more.
THOMAS ELLIOTT. I am an officer. I took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. Mrs. Elliott authorised me to give the trunk to another person.
SARAH ELLIOTT. No, I did not - I said no person should take it but the man that brought it; I thought there was some mistake about it.
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Confined Six Months.

259. GEORGE PETERS was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of November, 1 batten, value 1s. 6d., the goods of William Reed.
WILLIAM REED. I live in Hare-street, Bethnal-green, and am a weaver. I lost a batten on the 2nd of November, and found it at Mr. Cluff's: I had seen the prisoner before.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When had you seen it? A. I left it safe at a quarter before twelve o'clock, when I went out.
WILLIAM CLUFF. I keep a broker's-shop in Cheshire-street. On the 2nd of November, about twenty minutes past twelve o'clock, the prisoner brought a pair of battens; he asked 1s. for them - I did not know they were stolen; I went out from two to four, and when I came home there were fifty people round my house.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you deal in a great variety of articles? A. Yes; I bought the battens of the prisoner, in the name of William Jones - I pay ready money; William Cooper is my servant, and was by my side when I bought them: the prisoner was bailed by the Magistrate.
WILLIAM COOPER. I am servant to Mr. Cluff. On the 2nd of November the prisoner sold the battens to my master - he asked 1s. for them, which my master gave him; I had not known him before; my master put it down in the book, "Jones 1s."
Cross-examined. Q.Is the book here? A. No - this was about twenty minutes after twelve o'clock; I was not at home when they were claimed.
SAMUEL MOORE. I am an officer. I took the prisoner about four o'clock on the 2nd of November.
Cross-examined. Q.What day of the week was it? A. On Wednesday, Mr. Reed gave me charge of him - he was bailed by the Magistrate.
Witness for the Defence.
ELIZABETH MILLER. On the 2nd of November I was at work at No. 101, Green-street; the prisoner came there at half-past ten o'clock that morning - he brought home a bird-cage which I had lent him; he left my place from thirty to thirty-five minutes after eleven - Cheshire-street is about a mile and a quarter from there.
HENRY BLUMSER. I am a silk-mercer, and live at No. 1, Bacon-street, Bethnal-green. On Wednesday, the 2nd of November, I saw the prisoner sitting at his father's table, at dinner, at ten minutes past twelve o'clock; his father lived at No. 3, Cheshire-street - I staid with him till five minutes past one; he did not leave the room the whole of that time, upon my oath.
NOT GUILTY.

260. JOHN LODGE was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of November, 3 half-crowns, 4 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Mary Ann Lodge; and 1 pair of shoes, value 3s., the goods of Emma Lodge.
MARY ANN LODGE. I am the prisoner's sister. I live with my mother, but not my father; the prisoner has been very much neglected by his father for the last three years, and has lived with us - I had some money in a trunk in the room the prisoner slept in, on the 24th of November; he got up that morning before seven o'clock, and went away: my sister and I had been up at work till two in the morning, and we did not get up till eight - I then missed three half-crowns, four shillings, and one sixpence of mine, and a pair of shoes of my sister's; I went and found him at the top of Golden-lane, between eight and nine o'clock the same evening.
EMMA LODGE. I am the prisoner's sister. These are my shoes, and I know the money was in the trunk.
JAMES BUCKLE I am an officer. The witnesses sent for me, and gave the prisoner into custody; he had a new jacket on, which he said he bought in Golden-lane, and he said that he had bought a pair of trousers which he gave to another boy, and the rest of the money he had spent in a coffee shop - he said he might as well take his sisters money as any body's; he has been dreadfully neglected: the two young woman bear good characters, and one of them left her place, at Mr. Baker's, to attend to the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Judgment Respited .

361. JAMES HEALE and GEORGE CHAPMAN were indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of November, 1 handkerchief, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Nicholas Brown.
ALICE BROWN. I am the wife of Nicholas Brown - I take in washing, and live in North Wharf-road, Paddington. On the 3rd of November, about three o'clock in the afternoon, I was at my window, and saw the prisoners go by together, and as they passed, Chapman put his hand through the palings, and took down a handkerchief, and the peg flew out - Heale was close to him; I followed them, and saw Heale putting something into his bosom -I said, "Give me the handkerchief," but they got away too fast for me to follow them; I sent a lad after them, and they were taken.
WILLIAM TRENCH. I pursued the prisoner, and criedStop thief! I lost sight of them, but they were taken the same night.
RICHARD HANCOCK. I am a Police-officer. I saw the prisoner go past the prosecutor, but did not see them take any thing; the prosecutor gave me information; I described the prisoners to McLean.
Heale. You said it was not one of us. Witness. When I was fetched to the watch-house, you were laying down, and I did not know you, but I could swear to Chapman.
DUNCAN McLEAN. I am a Policeman. From the description given me by Hancock, I knew where to find the prisoners, and took them the same evening.
Chapman's Defence. I was not near the place. I had not been a quarter of an hour with Heale when we were taken.
HEALE - GUILTY. Aged 18.
CHAPMAN - GUILTY. Aged 19.

3rd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of Rowland Wilks.
AMBROSE TIDMAN. I live in Hackney-road. I had the care of an empty house; the different persons it belonged to, (who are named in the indictment,) agreed to put me in possession of it, as the ownership was disputed- on the 30th of November I met the prisoner coming out of the house with two sashes in his hand, which I had seen secure the day before, and fixed; I asked what he was going to do with them - he said to get them repaired; the door had been wrenched from the hinges, and taken away, but he had not business there.

265. WILLIAM RANDALL was indicted for stealing on the 24th of November, 6 spoons, value 3l. 3s., the goods of Bezzett Doveton.
JEMIMA BENTLEY. I am in the service of Bezzett Doveton, Esq., of Dorset-place. On the 24th of November I was coming from the drawing-room; I stopped in a little room, where the lady's maid and the nursery-maid sat at work - I heard a noise down stairs; I ran down, and at the butler's pantry, where the plate is kept, I saw the prisoner, who was a stranger; I asked what business he had there - I callede him a thief, and said, "You are after the plate;" he said, "I am not after the plate - I came to see whether the chimneys want sweeping or dusting;" I said, "Why did not you knock at the door?" he said he got his bread in an honester way than thieving - he took off his jacket, and shook it; I was simple enough to let him go - when he was gone I counted the plate, and missed all these soons - I ran up the steps, and saw him turn into Park-street; I went and got the officer to follow him - he brought him back, but the spoons are lost; I am certain he is the man - the spoons were all safe when he came to our house.
SUSAN BLADWIN. I am nursery-maid at the house - I saw the prisoner take off his jacket, and shake it; we then counted the plate, and the spoons were missing.
RICHARD AMEY. I am a Police-officer. Our serjeant gave me a description of the prisoner, and I took him; he said he was not the man, and asked me to let him call his brother - he called his brother, who was not at all like him, and the prisoner got away.
THOMAS ARNOLD. I am a Police-officer. I took the prisoner on Saturday morning at his brother's house, in William-street; when I inquired for him a woman said he was not there, but I searched the house, and found him there asleep - he denied all knowledge of the plate.
CHARLES LAUGHTON. I went with Arnold, and took the prisoner at his brother's; what he has stated is correct.
Prisoner's Defence. On the 24th of November I was loading same soot away, and was in doors from half-past two o'clock till half-past four; I then went home, and was at home all night - I went out next morning as usual; I was not out of the way.
GUILTY. Aged 27. - Transported for Seven Years.

NOT GUILTY.

268. JAMES JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, 30lbs. of spelter, value 5s., the goods of the London Dock Company .
JAMES GRIFFIN. I work in the London-docks. On the 25th of October, about half-past eight o'clock, I saw the prisoner go into the spirit shed; he found the spelter, took some of it, and put it under his trousers; he then went to a pile of wood - I watched him for about ten minutes; Mr. Scott, the warehouse-keeper, came by, and I told him - I then went and asked him what he had done with the spelter - he denied having had any; I told Mr. Scott where I had seen him go; he went and found this spelter there - here are 16 lbs. of it: I have seen the prisoner about the docks, but I do not know that he worked there.
JOHN BARNES. I found the spelter behind the pile of wood in the yard.
DANIEL CLEMENTS. I am in the service of the Dock Company. On the morning of the 25th of October, a little before nine o'clock, the prisoner was brought up to the watch-house, with this piece of spelter - he never worked there; the shed where the spelter was was open, and he might make a passage through there, but he had no right there; I have no doubt of this being the property of the London Dock Company.
GUILTY. Aged 60. - Confined Three Months.

269. SARAH JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of November, 1 cloak, value 7s.; 1 pair of stays, value 5s.; 2 shawls, value 6s., and 1 bonnet, value 3s., the goods of Margaret Thomas.
MARGARET THOMAS. I live in Five Inkhorn-court, Whitechapel; I had known the prisoner eight days. She came to me 28th of November, and said she had a brother who was a bricklayer, and was very well off, in London, but she had lost his direction, and was very much distressed; I took her into my house as a servant - I sell milk; she staid eight days, and then left me without notice; she went out to the barn as usual, and did not return - I missed these things from the room I slept in.
GEORGE SMITH. I am a special-constable, at Shoreditch workhouse. On the 26th of October the prisoner was admitted into the house as a stranger, and on the Monday Mrs. Thomas came to inquire if we had such a person - she claimed the property.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I was so much abused that I took any thing I could get hold of, and left my own things there.
MARGARET THOMAS. She did leave her clothes, but I think they were not so good as mine.
NOT GUILTY.

270. PETER HART was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of November, 1 coat, value 8s., the goods of George Donalds; and 1 pair of pattens, value 1s., the goods of Thomas Liddington.
GEORGE DONALDS. I lodge at Mrs. Walker's, in Weather-court, Whitecross-street. I put away my coat in the drawer on the 21st of November, and on the 22nd it was missing - the prisoner lodged in the same room with me; I saw my coat again at Worship-street on the Thursday following; the prisoner had lodged there about three months.
MARY LIDDINGTON. I am the wife of Thomas Liddington, and lodge in Phoenix-place, Old-street. On the 21st of November I went to Mrs. Walker's, and left my pattens there - when I called again they were gone.
MARY WALKER. I keep the house - Donalds lodged there. On the 21st he came down, and put his coat into the drawer as usual - the next morning I heard a quarrel up stairs about missing some things; I then opened the drawer, and missed the coat - I went up, and said, "What have you done with the coat?" Donalds said, "I put it into the drawer;" they then came down, and the prisoner said, "Never mind; I took it - I will make it all right on Saturday night."
JOHN WILLIAM FRYET. I am a pawnbroker. I have a coat, pawned in the name of John Walker, on the 21st of November, and, to the best of my belief, by the prisoner.
JOHN HARRISON CLARKE. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Old-street. I have a pair of pattens, pawned on the 22nd of November, by a man, in the name of Peter Littleton.
JOHN SLATER. I took the prisoner, and was going tosearch him, when he put the duplicates of these things on a chair.
The prisoner received a good character.

271. JOHN FREEGROVE was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of December, 1 waistcoat, value 4s., the goods of Mary Ann Ridley.
MARY ANN RIDLEY. I am a widow, and live in Little George-street. The prisoner came this day fortnight to purchase a waistcoat, and I showed him several - he came again on the 2nd of December with a young man; they asked for waistcoats, and I gave each one to try on - my daughter was in the shop; I went out, and when I returned she said to me, "Mother, they have put something into their hats;" I said, "Young men, if you are honest, you will pull off your hats;" the other one ran away, but the prisoner was detained - when the Policeman came he threw down his hat in the shop, and in it was this waistcoat - I believe the other man took one, but I would not swear it.
EPHRAIM PITT. I am a Police-constable. I was sent for, and found the prisoner; he had thrown down his hat before I got there.(Property produced and sworn to)
LOUISA RIDLEY. I have heard what my mother has stated, and it is correct - I saw the waistcoat in the prisoner's hat - he said if I got it out he would kick me, which he did.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, declaring his innocence.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18311201-272]
272. JOHN CRAWLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of November, 1 bullock's head, value 2s. 6d., and 1 iron hook, value 1s., the goods of Joseph Harradence.
JOHN SLATER. I am a Police-constable. I met the prisoner at half-past six o'clock, on the 21st of November, in South-street, carrying a bullock's head on his shoulder- I said, "What have you got there?" he said he had bought it, but he would not tell me where; I took him to the station-house - a boy brought in this iron hook, and said he had dropped it.
JOSEPH HARRADENCE. I am a butcher, and live in Chiswell-street. I saw the head at the station-house, and knew it to be mine; I had missed that and the hook from my shop - I know nothing of the prisoner; he was rather intoxicated.
GUILTY. Aged 34. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-273]
273. ANN CLAPTON was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of November; 1 necklace, value 10s., the goods of Edward Nelson.
EDWARD NELSON. I live in West-row, Carnaby-market - the prisoner has lodged in the house for eight or nine months. My wife was ill, and the prisoner tendered her services to her; I believe her husband is a carpenter- she was in my wife's room about an hour; she was the first person that came out of the room - I had seen this necklace in the room just before, and was informed it was gone - I then went in, and missed it; it had hung on an image, over the mantel-piece.
JOSEPH SMITH. I am a pawnbroker. I received this necklace from the prisoner on the 29th of November.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18311201-274]
274. MARY BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of December, 5 sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, and 3 sixpences, the monies of Matthew Rutherford, from his person.
MATTHEW RUTHERFORD. I am a seaman; I had been to the Commons, and received my wages on the 2nd of December, and met the prisoner in a coffee-shop in Ratcliff-highway, between one and two o'clock the next morning: I had then fourteen sovereigns, and I changed one - I was not sober when I got to the coffee-shop; I found the prisoner there: she accosted me - she asked for something to drink - I gave her half a crown: she got some rum, and brought me three sixpences in change - I then went with her to her own house: I was there a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes; I counted my money in her house, but I did not give her any thing: I had nothing to do with her - she put her hands into my pocket, when I sat down on the chair; I said, "This will not do for me;" I got up and counted my money, I found I had still my thirteen sovereigns, and I put them all safe into my left-hand breeches pocket; I bade her good night, and was going down - as soon as I got to the stair-head, she came and put her hand into my pocket; I seized her hand, and got eight sovereigns out of it - she got off with the rest and went out; I went out of the house in about five minutes, and found her in custody; I had not given her any money.
JAMES GRAHAM. I am a Police-constable. I heard a noise, and hastened to the place; I heard the prisoner run up some steps a little way from the house - she said, "Oh God! I have lost my shoe;" she came running towards me; I had a lamp in front of me - I turned the light on, and saw one of her hands grasped; I caught at it, and, to the best of my belief, I heard three sovereigns fall - I then searched her, and found two half-crowns and three sixpences in her bosom; I searched the place and so did others, but we found no sovereigns; the prosecutor said,"That is the girl that robbed me of five sovereigns, two half-crowns, and three sixpences."
Prisoner. Q. Did I not tell you, that he gave me two half-crowns and three sixpences? A. No, you never said any such thing; another officer found 8 1/2d. in her pocket.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that the prosecutor had given her the money.
GUILTY. Aged 38. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-275]
275. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of December, 1 cloak, value 20s., the goods of Thomas Collins.
SARAH ANN RISEBOROUGH. I was in Mr. Collins' shop, opposite the Grove, at Hackney, on the 1st of December, and saw the prisoner take a cloak from the door, and run away with it; I informed the shopman, who pursued - he was taken to the station-house; I saw him there, and can positively state he is the man.
ROBERT TURNER FRY. On the 1st of December I saw the prisoner running from the door; I followed him, and called Stop thief! he then turned a corner, and I lost sightof him - he was then stopped by a gentleman; I saw him throw the cloak away - it was brought to me by Tyndale.
JOHN RENSHAM. I was walking up the Grove, and saw several persons struggling; I made up to them - Fry gave me the prisoner and this cloak.
THOMAS TYNDALE. I was at work in the garden where the cloak was thrown; I took it up and gave it to Fry.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I have always got my living in an honest way, and hope this will be a warning to me.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18311201-276]
276. JOHN WELSH and THOMAS KELLY were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of December, 3 lbs. of cheese, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Thomas Gardener.
THOMAS GARDENER. I live in Somer's-town, and am a cheesemonger. I missed a piece of cheese on the 2nd of December, at a quarter-past five o'clock; I looked out, and saw the prisoners in a court near my shop; and at half-past five Welsh came and took another piece; I ran out, and took Kelly at the corner of the court - this piece of cheese fell from his coat; I had seen him and Welsh in company together - I then took both the prisoners to the station-house.
ROBERT WOOLEY. I am a Police-constable. I received the prisoners, and have the cheese; I asked Kelly how he got it - he said Welsh took it, and gave it him.
WELCH - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Whipped and Discharged.
KELLY - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-277]
277. BENJAMIN MORRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of November, 1 snuff-box, value 14s., the goods of George John William Ruthven.
GEORGE JOHN WILLIAM RUTHVEN. My father set me up in business as a tobacconist - my shop is at No. 16, Wych-street. On the 8th of November the prisoner came and asked for half an ounce of returns - I served him; he gave me a shilling, and I gave him change - he then asked to look at a snuff-box in the window - I showed him one, and said the price was a guinea; he then asked to look at another, which was 16s. - he put on the lid, and ran away with it; there was another person with him, and we think he run away with another box - my uncle followed the prisoner, and just as he got to the New-church, in the Strand; the Policeman took him.
THOMAS TIPPER. I am a Police-constable. On the 8th of November I heard a cry of Stop thief! in Newcastle-street - I followed the prisoner, who was running, and as I was bringing him back a lad gave me this snuff-box, which he said he saw the prisoner throw away.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. It is my first time of being here.
GUILTY. Aged 24. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18311201-278]
278. ELIZABETH LONG was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of November, 2 shirts, value 4s.; 1 pair of drawers, value 1s.; 1 waistcoat, value 3s.; 1 hat, value 3s., and 1 handkerchief, value 6d., the goods of John Harris.
JOHN HARRIS. I am a seaman On the 19th of November I went with a messmate to the White Swan, in Lower Shadwell; I had a bundle, containing this property - the prisoner came into the White Swan, and I treated her with a glass of spirits; I went out, and when I returned she and my messmate, and my bundle were all gone - I am sure she is the woman.
GEORGE RIDDELL. I keep the public-house. The prosecutor came in with the prisoner and another sailor; the prosecutor had a bundle - he went out for a short time, and while he was gone I saw the bundle under the prisoner's arm - she and another female left the house with the shipmate, but whether she took it out I cannot say: the prosecutor came in and asked for his shipmate - I said they had all left.
Prisoner's Defence. I had the bundle, but did not take it out.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18311201-279]
OLD COURT. THURSDAY, DECEMBER 8.
Second Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
279. THOMAS HAGUE was indicted for perjury.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS and PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
The following Documents were put in and read:-Two affidavits made by the defendant, sworn on the 25th of July, 1829, and the 4th of February, 1831, in the cause of Hague against Drury - in the latter of which the defendant had deposed that George Vandepant Drury was indebted to him in the sum of 500l. for goods sold and delivered by the deponent, to the said George Vandepant Drury, and at his request - a writ for 500l., returnable on the 15th of April - and a warrant dated the 5th of February, 1831 - the copy of a nonsuit - and the declaration which stated that the plaintiff, Thomas Hague, had become acquainted with various matters and things connected with the defendant (George Vandepant Drury) which if generally known, would be very prejudicial to the defendant, and that upon his undertaking not to disclose the same, the defendant had undertaken to pay him a reward of 500l.; in another count it stated the amount to be due for work and labour - there were other counts stating it to be for money lent, advanced, laid out, &c.; the plaintiff was nonsuited, and to pay costs, 32l. 10s. - the bill of particulars of the plaintiff's demand, dated the 31st of May, 1831, stated it to be for 500l., pursuant to an agreement or promise made to or with the defendant in 1829 - another bill of particulars, dated the 11th of June, 1831, stated it to be "the balance of an account as stated and settled with the defendant."
- DEVERELL. I was attorney for the defendant in this action; he is the prosecutor to-day - I attended a Judge of the Court of King's Bench, upon the delivery of the second particulars of demand - the agent to the plaintiff's attorney also attended; I said the general item as to the balance must be struck out, unless the account was furnished, to which the Judge agreed; they delivered no account at any time, but a third bill of particulars was delivered with that item struck out. On the 15th of June Mr. Justice James Parke made a rule to strike out that item.
GEORGE VANDEPANT DRURY, ESQ. I am the prosecutor of this indictment, and am the grandson of the late Sir George Vandepant; I spent the early part of my life in the East Indies, till 1806 - I then returned to England,and married a lady in Yorkshire; I went with her to India, and came back in 1813 - I resided at a place called Skelton, in Yorkshire, up to March, 1830, being absent occasionally. I first saw the defendant about thirteen years ago; I saw him as any other person about my house - he then lived at Rawcliffe, a village near me; I was arrested at his suit for an alleged debt of 130l. in 1829 - that was settled; he afterwards commenced a suit for 500l.; I put in bail to that, without being arrested - I never had any business transaction with him, and never purchased any thing of him; I swear that positively - I remember in 1824 his acting as agent to his mother, who kept a little farm near my residence; I purchased 5l. or 6l. worth of straw of her that year, and he acted as her agent - I had no other dealings whatever with him; I paid for that straw.
Q. I need hardly ask you, whether up to this instant you ever owed him for goods sold or delivered, or for any thing, the sum of 500l? A. I never did, I swear so most positively; I am living on my fortune, the result of my service in India.
Cross-examined by MR. ALLEY. Q. Do not you know that the defendant has a farm, for which he pays 200l. a year rent? A. He had nothing to do with the farm - it was his mother's; she is now dead: the defendant is a member of the yeomanry corps of the County, which is commanded by Lord Grantham; I have heard he was an overseer of the poor - the action for 130l. was settled before the declaration was filed; I paid the costs.
Q. Was there not a receipt in full, demanded of the defendant, before the money was paid, and did not he refuse to give it? A. Not before the money was paid; I only know that from what I learned from those who were engaged in the business - I instructed my attorney to demand a receipt in full, but never got it; I had paid him money before the 130l. in 1825 and 1826, under circumstances of threats; I paid him various sums - I paid him 200l. at Aberford, in 1825.
Q. Did you happen to meet him on the 7th of November, 1824, at a place, not at your house? A. I did on the road to York, in a field - I had a person with me; I was walking - I had not been sitting down at all; the person with me was the defendant's brother - he is now living: I have not brought him here - I did not request the defendant to call at my house the next day; my brother-in-law went to him with that request, in consequence of the representation I made to him - my brother-in-law is not here: I saw the defendant at my house on the afternoon of that day - nobody was present at the interview, not even my brother-in-law.
Q. Did you not request the defendant, for God's sake, not to repeat to any body what he had seen the day before, but to keep it secret? A. I did not, on my oath - I positively swear I did not tell him I must make him a friend, and if he would keep it secret I would introduce him among gentlemen in the country.
Q. Am I to understand you to swear you did not at that time solicit him to keep a secret for you? A. He obtained money from me - from the nature of his threats, I paid him money; I did not solicit him to keep a secret - nothing was said to him about keeping a secret; I swear that positively: there was a Bible in the room, and it might be on the table - there were very likely other books there, for the conversation was in my study.
Q. Did you not at that time ask him to swear on the Bible that he would keep the secret? A. When he had received the money - he had received the money - I do not recollect whether it was before he had received it, that I asked him.
Q. Did he swear on the Bible? A. He might have done so - Yes, he did; he swore he would not mention any thing which, of course, would have affected my character.
Q. Any thing he had seen the day before? A. Any thing he might devise for money; he swore he would not make any more demands for money; this was in November, 1824 - my brother-in-law, Mr. Thompson, was in the neighbourhood at the time; the prisoner was there a very short time - I did not ask him to take some wine: he might be treated hospitably, as any other person, by my servants; he was not treated with cake and wine - he lived about three-quarters of a mile off; I did not put any thing into his pocket - I put the money into his hand - I keep a butler, footman, and other servants.
Q. Did you happen to leave home at that time for a few months? A. I was going a journey; I went to Scarborough, with a niece who was ill - I was there about two months; I returned home for a few days - my niece asked me to accompany her to the South, and I went; on my return home, I stopped at the Morris Arms, Aberford, a few miles from my house - it is the principal inn; my lady and niece were with me - I did not send for the prisoner to come there to me; I waited for him - I found him there; I had not sent him word that I was coming home - I was not in correspondence with him.
Q. Did he not come there in consequence of an invitation - no matter how it was conveyed? A. Yes - I saw him in the principal sitting-room; my niece was walking in the garden; my wife was present.
Q. Did you not there, in the presence of your wife, request him to take an oath that he would still keep the secret? A. I made no request - he did take an oath; the Bible might be in one corner of the room, or probably it was on the table: I did not promise to give him 250l., and promise a further payment of 500l. to him - I did not prosecute him for extorting the money in 1824; I had a little boy named Cooper in my service, he is the son of a poor woman in the neighbourhood: he lived with me about seven years, and left to better his situation; my wife is here. On my oath, I did not request the prisoner, that if he should arrest me for 500l., to say that it was for goods sold and delivered; I did not see him, and never said a word on the subject to him, nor has any body by my authority - I have not seen him to speak to him for three years and a half.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. In what state of health is your brother-in-law, Mr. Thompson, at this instant? A. In a most wretched state of health - he generally has very bad health; I do not think he could attend here without considerable risk to himself in a case like this: this is the receipt for the 130l. - (read)
King's Bench, Hague against Drury - Received on the 9th of November, 1829, of the above-named defendant, by payment of Mr. Russel, 140l., in discharge of the debt in this action, brought to recover the balance of a promissory note, drawn by the defendant for 250l.
Q. Now, on your oath, had you ever given that man any promissory note whatever? A. No - before the receiptwas given, a demand was made on the defendant to give up the promissory note, if he had one; I never received one.
Q. You have been asked if you implored the defendant never to mention what he had seen - had you done anything that you was afraid of being seen or told of? A.Certainly not, he had no secret to keep if he told the truth; I gave him the money because I dreaded even the name of what he threatened, which was to charge me with an unnatural crime with his (Hague's,) own brother; there is not the shadow of a foundation for such a charge; I did not prosecute him, as I acted entirely from beginning to end under the advice of a solicitor in the country; (looking at two letters) these were received by Mrs. Drury; I know sufficient of the defendant's hand-writing to swear they are his - these other two are also his. - (read.)
York, 2nd of June, 1829.
To MR. DRURY. - The time has now elapsed) which will render all further correspondence in private; between you and me, unnecessary; you will find by the enclosed, its meaning - I have been this morning to Mr. Preston, to prevent all unpleasant consequences, but he has given me a determined refusal; I have asked, at your hands only, a favor, which I care not if all your family were witness to - I have no clandestine secret in view, but what I care not if all the world knew; I am sorry it comes to pass under your present family affliction, but I must take care of myself - I shall expect a personal interview to-morrow; coming to your house, under present circumstances, I do not wish, unless compelled to do so. THOMAS HAGUE.
Q. What family affliction were you enduring at that time? A. My niece, whom we had taken to the South, had died; we had numerous afflictions, and some deaths in the family - (the following letter was here read.)
2nd of February, 1830.
To MRS. DRURY. - Honored Madam - It is with the utmost reluctance I again address you, particularly when I think of the handsome manner your generous conduct has been to me; I have this moment received a letter from a corn-factor at Leeds, remitting me 30l. instead of 50l., and our rent day being next Thursday, I have no means of raising the amount without assistance: it will greatly oblige me, if Mrs. Drury will add another obligation to those already conferred by the sum of 12l., on or before Saturday, at the post-office, York, till called for; to see you is impossible, for I cannot ask for it personally, after what has transpired.
MR. DRURY. Mrs. Drury has been subpoenaed here to day on the part of the defendant, and is in attendance; with the exception of 5l. or 6l. which I paid the prisoner for his mother's straw, I never paid him any other money, except under threats; he obtained 120l. at my house, and gave a promissory note for the re-payment - I came here last Session, prepared to prosecute this indictment; the defendant, from first to last, has had some thousands from me under threats.
MR. ALLEY. Q.Where is the 120l. note of hand which he gave? A. It is mislaid; it was given three years and a half or four years ago; the letter written to my wife was not in answer to one written by her to him, by my desire - I was out; I am a native of Suffolk, but have lived in Yorkshire from 1813.
MR. DEVERELL. I am attorney for the prosecution, in the case of Hague against Drury, on which the affidavit in question arose; I was not attorney in the first cause - I did not know Mr. Drury then; the cause in question stood for trial at the last Lent Assizes at York - here is a letter, dated the 17th of July, 1831, which has been proved to be the prisoner's hand-writing; it is dated on the Sunday previous to the day on which the cause was to come on - (read.)
To HENRY THOMPSON, ESQ. - Sir, - I have this moment received a note from Mr. Anderson, requesting me to send him the letters I have in my possession, from Mr. and Mrs. Drury, to be at his office this evening at six o'clock; I think you know so much of this business, it is possible to satisfy both parties; without it coming into Court - if you will honour me with an interview before six o'clock this afternoon. I can give plenty of written demonstration to satisfy both parties; an answer by your servant will oblige. THOMAS HAGUE.
MR. DEVERELL. This letter was communicated to me by Mr. Thompson, about the middle of the day, on Sunday; I desired him to take no notice of it whatever, and I believe none was taken - I went to York fully prepared to try the cause, and delivered briefs to counsel; upon going into Court in the morning it was to be tried the record was drawn by the plaintiff - here is a note written by the defendant, the day after the record was drawn.(read.)
Tuesday morning, ten o'clock, 19th of July, 1831.
Thomas Hague begs leave to inform Henry Thompson, Esq. that a regular Bench-warrant has this morning been obtained against G. V. Drury, I give Henry Thompson, Esq., the information to use his discretion accordingly.
MR. DEVERELL. I have searched, and can hear nothing of a Bench-warrant or an indictment being preferred against Mr. Drury - I was first employed by him about February, 1830, since which he has acted entirely under my advice.
MR. ALLEY. Q. Are you an agent, or his attorney? A. No, he is my own client - I am an intimate friend of his nephew; Mr. Russell, of York, had been his attorney before.
WILLIAM HILL. I entered Mr. Drury's service, as butler, in April. 1819. It was my business to purchase the goods used on the farm, and in the house; the defendant's mother held a small farm under Lord Grantham - she has been dead about twelve months; the defendant and his brother attended to her business; in 1824, 5l. or 6l. worth of straw was bought off their farm; I always understood it had been paid for - I took the mother some money, and Thomas Hague came to offer the straw in part payment of 25l. which had been lent to him; nothing else has ever been purchased of Hague for my master - the defendant had no appearance of being worth money; he had two brothers and two sisters, and what the mother left, was between them - she had nothing but the farm, and a little property left by Mr. Ratey.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Do you recollect one night in November, 1824, the defendant calling when Mr. Thompson was there? A. He frequently called in an abusive manner; I and the other servants were about- I do not recollect Mr. Thompson ever going and fetching him back; I have occasionally carried letters to the defendant to the gate - when he has come with letters or messages, I have brought him out the answer.
Q. Did you not meet him when you were in a gig, and tell him Mrs. Drury wanted to see him? A. No, I met him once; he had borrowed some money of master, and I went to ask when he was going to pay it.
RICHARD LAWRENCE. I am in Mr. Drury's service, and have been so about thirteen years; I have known the prisoner about that time - I remember 5l. or 6l. worth of straw being bought once at the farm; nothing else was ever bought of him for our house.
THOMAS COWL. I keep a public-house, at Clifton, near York, about a mile and a half from the defendant - he has frequented my house for fifteen or twenty years; he was not able to pay his way - I have applied to him for payment of a score, which was never more than 2l., time after time; he once paid me 11s., which had been running eleven years.
Cross-examined by MR. ALLEY. Q. Did you ever hear that he had had thousands from the prosecutor? A. If he had one would have supposed he might have cleared a pound off his score, if he had been an honest man - he used to pay me by a little corn at a time.
An affidavit made on the part of the defendant to put off the trial last Session, on account of the absence of Mr. Russell, was here read, and Mr. Alley admitted that Mr. Russell was now in attendance.
MR. ALLEY addressed the Court and Jury on the part of the defendant.
GUILTY. Confined Six Months, to stand in the Pillory, and be Transported for Seven Years.

The prosecutor having brought an action against the prisoner for the recovery of this sum, MR. ADOLPHUS, on the part of the prosecution, declined proceeding.
